## 3.11 Summary and further reading

In this chapter, we introduced the category Poly , whose objects are polynomial functors and whose morphisms are the natural transformations between them. We call these natural transformations dependent lenses , or lenses for short. We also proved our fi rst categorical property of Poly : that it has all small coproducts.

The main result of this chapter was a concrete characterization of our dependent lenses between polynomial functors. A dependent lens : → is characterized by its

- on-positions function , a function that we denote by 1 : ( 1 ) → ( 1 ) sending -positions forward to -positions; and its
- on-directions functions , one for each -position that we denote by ♯ : [ 1 ] → [ ] [ ] [ ] sending 1 -directions backward to -directions.

This forward-backward relationship is what makes dependent lenses so well-suited for modeling interaction protocols . Given two agents with positions and directions, a dependent lens between them defines an interaction protocol that describes how the position of the fi rst agent determines the position of the second agent and how the direction of the second agent determines the direction of the fi rst. This perspective is exhibited by our corolla and polybox pictures for lenses. We studied examples of lenses between special polynomials: in particular, lenses between monomials are known as bimorphic lenses in functional programming literature.

We then unwound our interpretation of natural transformations between polynomials as dependent lenses with on-positions and ondirections functions to describe what happens to these functions when lenses compose. This gave us an accessible way to interpret commutative diagrams in Poly that is particularly convenient to express using polyboxes.

Finally, we considered various categorical structures on Poly , e.g. that it has all products and coproducts, and that these distribute. We also discussed how one can take any monoidal product ★ from Set and lift it to a monoidal product · on Poly :

![[Polynomial Functors_ A Mathematical Theory of Interaction.assets/formula-0146.png]]

A special case of this is the product structure × on Poly , which emerges from the coproduct structure + on Set . The other case of interest is the parallel (or Dirichlet) product structure ⊗ on Poly , which emerges from the product structure × on Set :

![[Polynomial Functors_ A Mathematical Theory of Interaction.assets/formula-0147.png]]

Variants of lenses are studied in compositional game theory [Hed+16; Hed17; Hed18a; Hed18b], in categorical database theory [JRW12], in functional programming and programming language theory [BPV06; OCo11; Abo+16], and in more generalized categorical settings [GJ12; Spi19].

## 4. Dynamical systems as dependent lenses

One of the main goals of this book is to use dependent lenses in Poly to model dynamical systems and automata. In this chapter, we will begin to see how to do this through an array of examples.

## 4.1 Moore machines

We start with our simplest example of a dynamical system: a deterministic state machine with a fi xed range of states, inputs, and outputs. At any point in time, this machine will inhabit one of its possible states and return output according to that current state. It can also update its current state according to the input it receives.

Definition 4.1 (Moore machine) . A Moore machine consists of the following data: three sets,

- a set , called the state-set , whose elements are states ;
- a set , called the position-set (or output-set ), whose elements are positions (or outputs );
- a set , called the direction-set (or input-set ), whose elements are directions (or inputs );

and two functions,

- return : → ;

- update: × → .

To emphasize the role that the three sets play, we can specify that this is an ( , ) -Moore machine with states .

The input and output terminology is standard, while the position and direction terminology is our own: we will soon see how the positions and directions of a Moore machine relate to that of a polynomial.

We should interpret an ( , ) -Moore machine as follows. At any time, the machine inhabits one of the states in its state-set . Say its current state is ∈ . We can ask the machine to perform one of the following two tasks.

- We can ask the machine to return its position : it should then produce the position return ( ) ∈ .
- We can feed the machine one of its directions ∈ and ask it to update its state : it should then replace its current state with the new state update ( , ) ∈ . Note that the new state depends not only on the direction the machine receives but also on the state the machine inhabits when it receives that direction.

We may visualize a Moore machine with a transition diagram as follows.

![[Polynomial Functors_ A Mathematical Theory of Interaction.assets/figure-0074.png]]

2. Following the red arrow from the bottom state, the machine updates its state to the left state.
3. At the left state, the machine returns the position 0.
4. Following the red arrow from the left state, the machine updates its state to-once again-the left state.
5. At the left state, the machine returns the position 0.
6. Following the blue arrow from the left state, the machine updates its state to the right state.
7. At the right state, the machine returns the position 1.
8. Following the red arrow from the right state, the machine updates its state to the left state.
9. At the left state, the machine returns the position 0.

. . .

In summary, starting from the bottom state, this Moore machine sends the sequence ( red , red , blue , red , . . . ) of directions in to the sequence ( 1 , 0 , 0 , 1 , 0 , . . . ) of positions in .

In general, given an initial state 0 ∈ , an ( , ) -Moore machine with states sends every sequence ( 1 , 2 , 3 , . . . ) of directions in to a sequence ( 0 , 1 , 2 , 3 , . . . ) of positions in , defined inductively as follows, via an intermediary sequence ( 0 , 1 , 2 , 3 , . . . ) of states in :

```
B return ( ) and + 1 B update ( , + 1 )
```

for all ∈ N . We will see that Poly gives us a more concise way to express this in Example 8.52.

Comparing Definition 4.1 with Example 3.41, we fi nd that an ( , ) -Moore machine with states is precisely a lens between monomials : y → y with on-positions function 1 B return: → and on-directions map ♯ B × → . The positions and directions of the Moore machine are the positions and directions of the codomain of the corresponding lens, while the domain of the lens has the states of the Moore machine as both its positions and its directions. So we can repackage Definition 4.1 as follows.

Definition 4.4 (Moore machine, version 2) . For , , ∈ Set , an

( , ) -Moore machine with states is a lens

![[Polynomial Functors_ A Mathematical Theory of Interaction.assets/formula-0199.png]]

## in Poly . We call

- the domain monomial y the machine's state system : its position-set (equivalently, its direction-set) is the machine's state-set , and its positions (equivalently, its directions) are the machine's states ;
- the codomain monomial y the machine's interface : its positionset and direction-set are the machine's position-set and directionset , and its positions and directions are the machine's positions and directions ;
- the on-positions function 1 : → the machine's return function ;
- the on-directions map ♯ : × → the machine's update function .

We call the codomain of a Moore machine its interface because it encodes how an outsider interacts with the machine: an outsider observes the positions of the interface that the machine returns and feeds the directions of the interface to the machine to update it. Rather than directly observing and altering the machine's states, an outsider must interact with the machine via its interface.

Here are some more examples of Moore machines.

Example 4.6 (Counter) . There is a ( 1 , N ) -Moore machine with states N that, with initial state 0 ∈ N , returns the sequence of natural numbers ( 0 , 1 , 2 , 3 , . . . ) . The machine is given by the lens N y N → N y whose on-positions function is the identity N → N and whose ondirections map N × 1  N → N sends ↦→ + 1. Here it is in polyboxes (recall that the shaded direction box indicates that the direction-set is a singleton, i.e. there is no choice to be made in filling it in):

![[Polynomial Functors_ A Mathematical Theory of Interaction.assets/figure-0075.png]]

The picture tells us that if the current state (the left position box) is ∈ N , the next state (the left direction box) is + 1 ∈ N . Since the machine just returns its current state as a position, the sequence of positions returned will always be an increasing sequence of consecutive natural numbers starting at the initial state.

Example 4.7 (Moving in the plane) . Let us construct a Moore machine with positions in R 2 , which we may think of as locations in the coordinate plane, and directions in [ 0 , ∞) × [ 0 , 2 ) , which we may think of as commands to move a certain distance ∈ [ 0 , ∞) at a certain angle ∈ [ 0 , 2 ) . We will let the machine's state-set be R 2 as well, so the machine is a lens

![[Polynomial Functors_ A Mathematical Theory of Interaction.assets/formula-0201.png]]

We can define such a lens using polyboxes:

![[Polynomial Functors_ A Mathematical Theory of Interaction.assets/figure-0076.png]]

Example 4.9 (Functions as memoryless Moore machines) . Given a function : → , there is a corresponding ( , ) -Moore machine with states that takes in an element of and returns the element of obtained by applying .

It is given by the lens y → y defined as follows:

![[Polynomial Functors_ A Mathematical Theory of Interaction.assets/formula-0202.png]]

That is, this lens is the identity on positions, returning the state directly as its position, and on directions it is the function × -→ 2 → -, which ignores the current state and applies to the direction received to compute the new state.

If the machine starts in state 0 and is given a sequence of directions ( 1 , 2 , . . . ) from , the machine will return the positions ( 0 , ( 1 ) , ( 2 ) , . . . ) . We say this machine is memoryless , because at no point does the state of the machine actually depend on any previous states; instead, its state depends only on the last direction it received.

Exercise 4.10 . Suppose we have a function : × → .

1. Find a corresponding ( , ) -Moore machine y → y . You may draw it out in polyboxes.
2. Would you say the machine is memoryless?

Exercise 4.11 . Find , ∈ Set such that the following can be identified with a lens y → y , and explain in words what the corresponding ( , ) -Moore machine does (there may be multiple possible solutions):

2. a magma , i.e. a set and a function × → ;
3. a set and a subset ′ ⊆ .

The previous examples of Moore machines mostly had identities as return functions. In the following exercises, we will build examples of Moore machines that do not return their entire states as positions.

Exercise 4.12 (Robot with health) . Think of the Moore machine in Example 4.7 as a robot and modify it as follows.

Add to its state a 'health meter,' which takes a real value between 0 and 1 representing the robot's health. Have the robot lose half its health each time it moves to a location whose -coordinate is negative. Do not return the robot's health; instead, use its health ℎ as a multiplier, allowing it to move a distance of ℎ given an input of .

Exercise 4.13 (Tape of a Turing machine) . A Turing machine has a tape consisting of a cell for each integer. Each cell bears a value ∈ B { 0 , 1 , -} , and one of the cells ∈ Z is distinguished as the 'current' cell. So the set of states of the tape is Z × Z .

The Turing machine interacts with the tape by asking for the value of the current cell, an element of ; and by changing the value of the current cell before moving left (i.e. replacing the current cell ∈ Z with the new cell -1) or right (i.e. replacing with + 1). Hence the tape's position-set is and its direction-set is × { left , right } .

1. If we model the tape as a Moore machine : y → y , what are , , and ?
2. Write down the specific that makes it act like a tape as specified above.

Exercise 4.14 (File-reader) . Say that a fi le of length is a function : n → ascii , where ascii B 256 . We refer to elements of n = { 1 , . . . , } as entries in the fi le and, for each entry ∈ n , the value ( ) ∈ ascii as the character at entry .

Given a file , design a file-reading Moore machine whose position-set is ascii + { done } and whose direction-set is

![[Polynomial Functors_ A Mathematical Theory of Interaction.assets/formula-0203.png]]

Given a direction ( , ) , the fi le-reader should go to entry in the file and return the character at that entry. If the given direction is instead continue , the fi le-reader should move to the next entry (i.e. from to + 1) and read that character-unless the new entry would be greater than , in which case the fi le-reader should return done until it receives another ( , ) pair.

While Exercise 4.14 gives us a functioning file-reader, it is rather awkward that we are still able to give the direction continue even when the position is done , or provide a new range of entries before the fi lereader has fi nished reading from the previous range. In Section 4.2, we will introduce a generalization of Moore machines to handle cases like these, where the array of directions the machine can receive changes depending on its current position. In particular, we will be able to let the fi le-reader 'close its port,' so that it cannot receive signals while it is busy reading, but open its port once it is done ; see Example 4.28.

## 4.1.1 Deterministic state automata

The diagram in Example 4.2 may look familiar to those who have studied automata theory; in fact, a deterministic state automaton can be expressed as a Moore machine with a distinguished initial state.

Definition 4.15 (Deterministic state automaton, language) . A deterministic state automaton consists of

- a set of states ;
- a set of symbols ;

- an update function : × → ;
- an initial state 0 ∈ ;
- a subset ⊆ of accept states .

Let

![[Polynomial Functors_ A Mathematical Theory of Interaction.assets/formula-0204.png]]

denote the set of fi nite sequences ( 1 , . . . , ) of symbols in ; we call such a sequence a word . We say that the automaton accepts the word ( 1 , . . . , ) if starting at the initial state and following the symbols in the word leads us to an accept state-or, more formally, if the sequence ( 0 , 1 , . . . , ) defined inductively by

![[Polynomial Functors_ A Mathematical Theory of Interaction.assets/formula-0205.png]]

is such that is an accept state:

∈ .

We call a subset of List ( ) a language , and we say that the set of all words in List ( ) that the automaton accepts is the language recognized by the automaton.

Remark 4.16 . When we study a deterministic state automaton, we are usually interested in which words the automaton accepts and, more generally, what language the automaton recognizes. While intuitive, the condition we provided for when an automaton accepts a word can be cumbersome to work with. In Example 8.51, we will give a more compact way of describing whether an automaton accepts a word and specifying the language the automaton recognizes. Better yet, we will find that this alternative formulation arises naturally from the theory of Poly .

Proposition 4.17. A deterministic state automaton with a set of states and a set of symbols can be identified with a pair of lenses

![[Polynomial Functors_ A Mathematical Theory of Interaction.assets/formula-0206.png]]

Proof. By Exercise 3.27, a lens y → y can be identified with an initial state 0 ∈ . Then a lens y → 2 y consists of a return function

: → 2 , which can be identified with a subset of accept states ⊆ , together with an update function : × → . □

In other words, we can think of a deterministic state automaton as a Moore machine with position set 2 along with a distinguished initial state; the Moore machine has the same states and update function as the automaton and the automaton's symbols as its directions.

Now imagine if we wanted to construct a version of this automaton that stops reading symbols (i.e. directions) whenever the machine enters an accept state (i.e. returns one position instead of the other). To do this would require a machine whose set of possible directions is dependent on its current position. Instead of an update function : × → , we would need an update function that takes a direction ∈ if the state ∈ is not an accept state (say, if ( ) = 1) but takes a direction in 0 (i.e. no direction) if the state is an accept state (if ( ) = 2). So there would be one update function : → if ( ) = 1 and a different update function → ( ) : 0 if = 2. But these are exactly the ondirections functions of a lens y → y + 1 ! Indeed, replacing our interface monomial with a general polynomial is exactly how we will obtain our generalized dependent Moore machines.

## 4.2 Dependent dynamical systems

Each of our Moore machines above has a monomial y as an interface. Every representable summand of such an interface has the same representing set , so the set of directions that can be fed into the machine is always . But by replacing y with an arbitrary polynomial , which may have a different direction-set at each position, we can model a broader class of machines.

Definition 4.18 (Dependent dynamical system) . A dependent dynamical system (or a dependent Moore machine , or simply a dynamical system ) is a lens

![[Polynomial Functors_ A Mathematical Theory of Interaction.assets/formula-0207.png]]

for some ∈ Set and ∈ Poly . We call

- the domain y the machine's state system : its position-set

(equivalently, its direction-set) is the machine's state-set , and its positions (equivalently, its directions) are the machine's states ;

- the codomain the machine's interface : its position-set and direction-sets are the same as the machine's position-set and direction-sets , and its positions and directions are the machine's positions and directions ;
- the on-positions function 1 : → ( 1 ) the machine's return function ;
- the on-directions map ♯ : [ 1 (-)] → the machine's update ♯ map , and the on-directions function : [ 1 ] → at ∈ the machine's update function at .

Example 4.19 (Dynamical systems as polyboxes) . We can express a dynamical system : y → in polyboxes as

![[Polynomial Functors_ A Mathematical Theory of Interaction.assets/figure-0077.png]]

We can visualize as a channel between the internal state system on the left and the external interface on the right. The state system enters its current state ∈ into the left position box, and the return function converts this state to a position ∈ ( 1 ) of the interface. At , the interface has a direction-set [ ] ; an interacting agent selects one of these directions ∈ [ ] to enter into the right direction box. Finally, the update map uses the current state and the position to fi ll the left direction box with the new state ∈ . Then the process repeats with in place of .

Remark 4.20 . It may seem limiting that the set of possible directions a dependent dynamical system can receive should depend on the current position rather than the current state ; but this makes sense philosophically if we accept that the system's interface should capture everything about how it interacts with the outside world. In particular, the system's position should capture everything an external observer could possibly perceive about the system, while the direction-set should capture all the ways in which an external agent can choose to interact with the system. But if the set of directions available to an external agent changes , the external agent should be able to detect this fact-the system's position must have changed as well! On the other hand, if the internal state changes, but the external position remains the same, the agent would see no difference-they would not know to interact with the system any differently, so the directions available to them would have to stay the same, too.

Here are some examples of dependent dynamical systems. We begin by fi nishing the example at the end of the last section.

Example 4.21 (Halting deterministic state automata) . Recall deterministic state automata from Definition 4.15. Say we want such an automaton to halt after reaching an accept state and read no more symbols. Then rather than a lens y → 2 y , we could use a lens

![[Polynomial Functors_ A Mathematical Theory of Interaction.assets/formula-0208.png]]

To give such a lens, we first need to provide a return function 1 : → { reject , accept } . We let send the accept states to accept and every other state to reject .

If we reach an accept state, we want the machine to halt. So at the position accept , corresponding to the summand 1 , there are no directions available. This makes the update function ♯ vacuous when 1 = accept .

On the other hand, when 1 = reject , the update functions ♯ : → specify how the machine updates its state for each direction in if the current state is . This corresponds to the automaton's update function.

When equipped with an initial state 0 ∈ specified by a lens y → y , we call these dependent dynamical systems halting deterministic state automata . Given a word ( 1 , . . . , ) ∈ List ( ) , we say that the automaton accepts this word if starting at the initial state and following the elements in the sequence leads us to an accept state, without reaching an accept state any earlier -or, more formally, if the sequence ( 0 , 1 , . . . , ) defined inductively by

![[Polynomial Functors_ A Mathematical Theory of Interaction.assets/formula-0209.png]]

is such that is the sequence's fi rst accept state:

![[Polynomial Functors_ A Mathematical Theory of Interaction.assets/formula-0210.png]]

We call the set of all words accepted by the automaton the language recognized by the automaton.

Remark 4.22 . Again, the conditions for when such an automaton accepts a word are rather awkward to formally state. We will see in Example 8.50 an alternative way of saying whether a word is accepted by a halting deterministic state automaton.

For further examples, every graph gives rise to a dynamical system; but to ensure that we are discussing the same concept, let us fi x the definition of a graph.

## Definition 4.25 (Graph) . A graph B ( ⇒ ) consists of

- a set of edges ;
- a set of vertices ;
- a source function : → that assigns each edge a source vertex;
- a target function : → that assigns each edge a target vertex.

So when we say 'graph,' we mean a directed graph, and we allow multiple edges between the same pair of vertices as well as self-loops.

Example 4.26 (Graphs as dynamical systems) . Given a graph B ( ⇒ ) with source and target functions , : → , there is an associated polynomial

![[Polynomial Functors_ A Mathematical Theory of Interaction.assets/formula-0211.png]]

Its positions are the vertices of the graph, and its directions at ∈ are the edges coming out of . We call this the emanation polynomial of .

The graph itself induces a dynamical system : y → , where ♯ 1 = id and = ( ) . So its states as well as its positions are the vertices of the graph, and a direction at a vertex ∈ is an edge ∈ coming out of that takes us from = ( ) along the edge to its target vertex ♯ = ( ) .

Exercise 4.31 . Modify the dynamical system from Example 4.30 as follows.

In the previous exercise, we added a reward system to the robot on the grid by manually redefining the associated lens. But there is a simpler way to think about the new system: it is the juxtaposition of two systems, a robot system and a reward system, in parallel. We will see how to express this in terms of lenses in Exercise 4.37, once we explain how to juxtapose systems like this in general in Section 4.3.2. In fact, we will see in Exercise 4.38 that the robot-on-a-grid system itself can be viewed as the juxtaposition of two systems, and this perspective will provide a structured way to generalize Example 4.30 to more than two dimensions.

## 4.3 New dynamical systems from old

We have seen how dependent dynamical systems can be modeled as lenses in Poly of the form y → . But we have yet to take full advantage of the categorical structure that Poly provides. In particular, based only on what we know of Poly so far from Chapter 3, we have three rather different ways of constructing new dynamical systems from old ones:

1. Given dynamical systems y → and y → , we can use the universal property of the categorical product to obtain a dynamical system y → × ; see Section 4.3.1.
2. Given dynamical systems : y → and : y → , we can take their parallel product to obtain a dynamical system ⊗ : y → ⊗ ; see Section 4.3.2.

3. Given a dynamical system : y → and a lens : → , we can compose them to obtain a dynamical system # : y → ; see Section 4.3.3.

Each of these operations has a concrete interpretation in terms of the systems' behavior. In this section, we will review each of them in turn.

## 4.3.1 Categorical products: multiple interfaces operating on the same states

Let be a set, and say that we have an -indexed family of dependent dynamical systems ( : y → ) ∈ that all share the same state-set . Then since Poly has all small products, the universal property of products induces a lens

![[Polynomial Functors_ A Mathematical Theory of Interaction.assets/formula-0217.png]]

which is itself a dynamical system with state-set . By (3.57), the interface of (i.e. the product that is its codomain) has position-set

![[Polynomial Functors_ A Mathematical Theory of Interaction.assets/formula-0218.png]]

and, at each position : ( ∈ ) → ( 1 ) , direction-set

![[Polynomial Functors_ A Mathematical Theory of Interaction.assets/formula-0219.png]]

We then characterize the dynamics of in terms of each by leveraging the universal property of products in Poly , detailed in the binary case in the solution to Exercise 3.64, as follows. The return function

![[Polynomial Functors_ A Mathematical Theory of Interaction.assets/formula-0220.png]]

sends each state ∈ to the dependent function ( ∈ ) → ( 1 ) sending ∈ to the position ( ) 1 returned by the corresponding dynamical system at the state . Then the update function

![[Polynomial Functors_ A Mathematical Theory of Interaction.assets/formula-0221.png]]

at ∈ sends each pair ( , ) in its domain, with ∈ and ∈ [( ) 1 ] , to where the update function of at sends the direction : namely ( ) ♯ . We can write using polyboxes as follows:

![[Polynomial Functors_ A Mathematical Theory of Interaction.assets/figure-0081.png]]

In other words, if there are multiple interfaces that drive the same set of states, we may view them as a single product interface that drives those states. This single dynamical system returns positions in all of the original systems at once; then it can receive a direction from any one of the original systems' direction-sets and update its state accordingly. It is as though all of the dynamical systems can detect the current state of the combined system, but only one of them can change the state at a time. So products give us a universal way to combine multiple polynomial interfaces into one.

## 4.3.2 Parallel products: Juxtaposing dynamical systems

Another way to combine two polynomials-and indeed two lenses-is by taking their parallel product, as in Definition 3.65. In particular, the parallel product of two state systems is still a state system. So parallel products give us another way to create new dynamical systems from old ones. The procedure is straightforward: take the product of the statesets as the new state-set, the product of the position-sets as the new position-set, and the product of the direction-sets at each of position in a tuple of positions as the new direction-set at that tuple.

For ∈ N , say that we have dynamical systems: a lens : y → for each ∈ n . Then we can take the parallel product of all of them to obtain a lens

![[Polynomial Functors_ A Mathematical Theory of Interaction.assets/formula-0222.png]]

By inductively applying Exercise 3.67, we fi nd that the domain of is

![[Polynomial Functors_ A Mathematical Theory of Interaction.assets/formula-0223.png]]

Î so is itself a dependent dynamical system with state-set ∈ n . Meanwhile, inductively applying (3.66) yields

![[Polynomial Functors_ A Mathematical Theory of Interaction.assets/formula-0224.png]]

as the position-set and, at each position ( ) ∈ n ,

![[Polynomial Functors_ A Mathematical Theory of Interaction.assets/formula-0225.png]]

as the direction-set of the interface of .

We can characterize the dynamics of in terms of each constituent dynamical system as follows. By our proof sketch of Proposition 3.73, the return function

![[Polynomial Functors_ A Mathematical Theory of Interaction.assets/formula-0226.png]]

sends each -tuple of states ( ) ∈ n in its domain, with each ∈ , to the -tuple of positions (( ) 1 ) ∈ n returned by each of the constituent dynamical systems at each state. Then at the -tuple of states ( ) ∈ Î ∈ n ∈ n , the update function

![[Polynomial Functors_ A Mathematical Theory of Interaction.assets/formula-0227.png]]

sends each -tuple of directions ( ) ∈ n in its domain, with each ∈ [( ) 1 ♯ ] (( ) ) , to the -tuple ∈ n consisting of states to which the update function of each at sends . We can write using polyboxes as follows:

![[Polynomial Functors_ A Mathematical Theory of Interaction.assets/figure-0085.png]]

In other words, multiple dynamical systems running in parallel can be thought of as a single dynamical system. This system stores the states of all the constituent systems at once and returns positions from all of them together; then it can receive directions from all of the constituent systems' direction-sets at those positions at once and update each constituent state accordingly. So parallel products give us a way to juxtapose multiple dynamical systems in parallel to form a single system.

## Exercise 4.38 .

1. Explain how the robot-on-a-grid dynamical system : y → from Example 4.30 can be written as the parallel product of some dynamical system with itself.
2. Use -fold parallel products to generalize Example 4.30 to robots on -dimensional grids.

Intuitively, the parallel product takes two dynamical systems and puts them in the same room together so that they can be run at the same time. But it does not allow for any interaction between the two systems. For that, we will need to use what we call a wrapper interface. We will introduce wrapper interfaces in the next section before describing how they can be used in conjunction with parallel products to model general interaction in Section 4.4.

## 4.3.3 Composing lenses: Wrapper interfaces

Given a dynamical system : y → , say that we want to interact with its state system using a new interface rather than . We can do this whenever we have a lens : → , which we could compose with our original dynamical system to obtain a new system y - → → -. We call the lens the wrapper and its codomain the wrapper interface , which we wrap around (or sometimes just , if a dynamical system has yet to be specified) using .

How does this new composite system # relate to the original dynamical system ? The lens converts a position from to a position 1 from ; at the same time, it allows the choice of direction from [ ] to depend on a choice of direction from [ 1 ] , converting directions of the wrapper interface to directions of the original interface . Precomposing with a dynamical system yields a new dynamical system that lets an agent interact with the original system using only this new interface wrapped around it.

arrows, yellow solid arrows, and red dotted arrows):

around using the following lens : → (with magenta dashed arrows, orange solid arrows, and lavender dotted arrows):

![[Polynomial Functors_ A Mathematical Theory of Interaction.assets/figure-0091.png]]

Each state returns a -position according to where the on-positions function of sends the -position that the state returns. Then each [ ] -direction is sent to a [ ] -direction via the on-directions function of at , and the update function of uses this [ ] -direction to compute the new state. So allows us to operate with the wrapper interface instead of the original interface .

4

## 4.3.4 Sections as wrappers

Say we wanted to model a dynamical system : y → within a closed system, for which an external agent can perceive no change in position and effect no change in direction. We can think of this as wrapping y , the interface with one position and one direction, around . To do so, we must specify a wrapper : → y . In the language of Definition 3.36, this is precisely a section of . As we noted then, this name is appropriate, since a way of sectioning off the interface from the outside world.

Recall that a section : → y can be identified with a dependent function of the form ( ∈ ( 1 )) → [ ] that sends each -position to a [ ] -direction, fi xing a direction at every position of . So a section for an interface dictates the direction it receives given any position it inhabits; there is no need for any further outside interference.

![[Polynomial Functors_ A Mathematical Theory of Interaction.assets/formula-0230.png]]

obtained by wrapping y around using .

Example 4.43 (The do-nothing section) . There is something rather off-putting about the way we model dynamical systems as lenses : y → . We know that sends states-as-positions to positions of the interface and, at each state-as-position, sends directions of the interface to states-as-directions. But we rely only on the labels of elements in to tell us which positions and directions refer to the same states!

Nothing inherent in the language of Poly makes these associations between states-as-positions and states-as-directions for us;

we have to rely on the position-set and direction-sets of the state system being the same set for the machine to work properly. Put another way, the monomials { 4 , 6 } y { 4 , 6 } , { 4 , 6 } y { 4 , 8 } , and { 3 , 5 } y { 6 , 7 } are all isomorphic in Poly , but the fi rst can be a state system while the other two cannot!

To address this issue, we need a way to connect the positions of a polynomial to its own directions in the language of Poly . This is where sections can help: a lens y → y is just a way of assigning to each position in a direction in . So we can define : y → y to be the section that sends each position ∈ to the direction at with the same name, corresponding to the same state. Note that can be identified with the identity function on (see Exercise 4.42). Now Poly knows for each position which direction is associated with the same state the position is.

In this way, we can generalize our notion of state systems to monomials y ′ equipped with a bijection → ′ , which we can then translate to a section : y ′ → y . But for convenience of notation, we will continue to identify the position-set of a state system with each of its direction-sets.

More concretely, the section : y → y acts as a very special (if rather unexciting) dynamical system: it is the do-nothing section , with only one possible position and one possible direction that always keeps the current state the same. While the system literally does nothing, we do know one key fact about it: given any state system, regardless of the state-set, we can always define a do-nothing section on it. 1

Yet this is not the whole story. The do-nothing section knows, at each position, the direction that keeps the system at the same state; but it does not know which of the other directions at that position correspond to which of the other states of the system. We are still relying on the labels of direction-sets being the same for that: for instance, the polynomials { 1 ′ , 2 ′ , 3 ′ } y { 1 , 2 , 3 } and { 1 ′ } y { 0 , 1 , 4 } + { 2 ′ } y { 2 , 5 , 6 } + { 3 ′ } y {-8 , -1 , 3 } are isomorphic, but even though each has a do-nothing section matching 1 ′ ↦→ 1 , 2 ′ ↦→ 2 , 3 ′ ↦→ 3 that makes the fi rst one into a state system, we do not have a way to tell Poly how to make the second one a state system yet.

From another perspective, : y → y does nothing, while : y → does 'one thing': it steps through the system once, producing the current state's position with the return function and taking in directions with the update function. It is ready to take another step, but how does Poly know which state to visit next? Is there a lens that does 'two things,' ' things,' or 'arbitrarily many things'? Can we actually run a dynamical system in Poly ? We will develop the machinery to answer these questions over the course of Part II, starting in Section 6.1.4.

## 4.4 General interaction

We are now ready to use Poly to model interactions between dependent dynamical systems that can change their interfaces and interaction patterns.

## 4.4.1 Wrapping juxtaposed dynamical systems together

When wrapper interfaces are used in conjunction with parallel products, they may encode multiple interacting dynamical systems as a single system. Explicitly, given ∈ N and a dynamical system : y → for each ∈ n , we can fi rst juxtapose them to form a single dynamical system # :

![[Polynomial Functors_ A Mathematical Theory of Interaction.assets/formula-0233.png]]

by taking their parallel product. Then we can wrap an interface Ë around using a wrapper : → ∈ , yielding a new composite n dynamical system

![[Polynomial Functors_ A Mathematical Theory of Interaction.assets/formula-0234.png]]

On positions, gives a way of combining all the positions of the constituent interfaces into a single position of the wrapper interface. On directions, takes into account the current positions of each constituent interface along with a direction for the wrapper interface to specify a direction for each of the constituent interfaces. In particular, a judiciously chosen on-directions function could feed positions of some interfaces as directions to others. When is a wrapper around a parallel product of interfaces, we call the interaction pattern between those interfaces.

Example 4.46 (Repeater) . Suppose we have a dynamical system : y → y + y that takes unchanging directions and sometimes returns elements of while other times returning only silence (the position associated with the right-hand summand y ). What if we wanted to construct a system that operates just like , but always returns elements of as output? Where would have returned silence, we want to instead repeat the last element of that was returned. (We allow to repeat an arbitrary element of if returns silence before it has returned any elements of yet.)

What we need is a way to store an element of and retrieve it as needed. So whenever returns an element of , we store it; then when returns silence, we retrieve the last element of we stored and return that instead.

What should this storage-retrieval dynamical system look like? It needs to take elements of as directions, return elements of as positions, and store elements of as states. In fact, the identity lens : y → y works perfectly: it returns the element of currently stored as its position and updates its state to the direction it receives.

Now we can juxtapose our original system with the storageretrieval system by taking their parallel product, yielding a dynamical system

![[Polynomial Functors_ A Mathematical Theory of Interaction.assets/formula-0235.png]]

that runs both systems in parallel: simultaneously and independently. But what we want is for and to interact with each other, and for the resulting system to only return elements of . To do so, we need to wrap an interface y around ⊗ by composing it with some lens

![[Polynomial Functors_ A Mathematical Theory of Interaction.assets/formula-0236.png]]

the interaction pattern between the interfaces y + y and y that we must define. Then we will be able to define B ( ⊗ ) .

# Since ⊗ distributes over + , by the universal property of the coproduct, it suffices to give lenses

![[Polynomial Functors_ A Mathematical Theory of Interaction.assets/formula-0237.png]]

The former corresponds to the case where returns an element of , while the latter corresponds to the case where is silent.

When returns an element of , we want the composite system to return that same element, but we also want to give that position as a direction to so that it can be stored. We do not need to do anything with the position returned by ; we can simply discard it. So should map ( , ′ ) ↦→ on positions, yielding the position returned by and discarding the position returned by ; and the on-directions function ♯ ( ′ ) : 1 → , should pick out the direction ∈ , feeding the position returned by as a direction for .

On the other hand, when returns silence, we want to return the position of instead. We also need to feed this position back to as a direction so that it can continue to be stored. So ℎ should be the identity on positions as well as the identity on directions.

Example 4.47 (Paddling) . We build a Moore machine with interface N y , interpreting the natural number position it returns as the machine's current location. Suppose we want to control how far the machine can move and what can make it move.

To model this, we introduce two intermediary systems, which we call the paddler and the tracker : 2

![[Polynomial Functors_ A Mathematical Theory of Interaction.assets/formula-0238.png]]

The paddler has interface 2 y because it is blind (i.e. takes no directions) and can only move its paddle (i.e. return a position) left or right : its position-set is 2  { left , right } . The tracker has interface 2 N y because it will return the location of the machine (as an element ∈ N ) as its position and take in the position of the paddler (as an element of 2 ) as its direction. We can wrap an interface N y around them both using an interaction pattern

![[Polynomial Functors_ A Mathematical Theory of Interaction.assets/formula-0239.png]]

whose on-positions function is the canonical projection 2 N → N , returning the location returned by the tracker, and whose ondirections map is the projection 2 N → 2 , passing the position of the paddler as a direction to the tracker.

We focus on the dynamics of the tracker. We want it to watch for when the paddle switches from left to right or from right to left , whereupon it should push the machine forward one unit. Thus the states of the tracker are given by B 2 N , storing which side the paddler is on and the machine's current location. The onpositions function of the tracker is the canonical projection 2 N → N that returns the current location; then at each ( , ) ∈ 2 N , the ondirections function of the tracker 2 → 2 N sends

![[Polynomial Functors_ A Mathematical Theory of Interaction.assets/formula-0240.png]]

storing the new position of the paddler as well as moving the machine forward one unit if the paddle switches while keeping the machine still if the paddle stays still.

Exercise 4.48 . Change the dynamics and state system of the tracker in Example 4.47 so that it exhibits the following behavior.

When the paddle switches once and stops, the tracker increases the location by one unit and stops, as before in Example 4.47. But when the paddle switches twice in a row, the tracker increases the location by two units on the second switch. So if the paddler is stable for a while, then switches three times in a row, the tracker will increase the location by one, then two, then two again.

2 Perhaps one could refer to the tracker as the demiurge ; it is responsible for maintaining the material universe.

## 4.4.2 Sectioning off juxtaposed dynamical systems

We saw in Section 4.3.4 that a section (i.e. lens to y ) for the interface of a dynamical system sections that dynamical system off as a closed system. So it should not come as a surprise that a section for a parallel product of interfaces yields an interaction pattern between the interfaces that only allows the interfaces to interact with each other, cutting off any other interaction with the outside world.

Example 4.51 (Picking up the chalk) . Imagine that you see some chalk and you pinch it between your thumb and forefinger. An amazing thing about reality is that you will then have the chalk, in the sense that you can move it around. How might we model this in Poly ? We will construct a closed dynamical system-one with interface y -consisting of only you and the chalk. To do so, we will provide an interface for you, and interface for the chalk, and a section for your juxtaposition.

Say that your hand can be at one of two heights, down or up , and that your fi ngers can either be pressed (with pressure between your thumb and forefinger) or unpressed . Say too that you take in information about the chalk's height, which can be down or up as well. Here are the two sets we will be using:

```
B { down , up } and B { pressed , unpressed } .
```

Your interface is y : your position is your own height and pressure, and your possible directions are the chalk's possible heights. As for the chalk, it is either in your possession or out of it as well as either down or up . The direction the chalk receives includes whether it is pressed or unpressed . When it's out of your possession, that is the entire direction, but when it is in your possession, its direction also comprises your hand's height. In summary, here are the two interfaces:

```
You B y Chalk B { out } y + { in } y . and
```

Now we want to give the interaction pattern between you and the chalk. As we said before, you see the chalk's height. If your hand is not at the height of the chalk, the chalk remains unpressed . Otherwise, your hand is at the height of the chalk, so the chalk receives your pressure (or lack thereof). Furthermore, if the chalk is in your possession, it also receives your hand's height.

To provide a lens : You ⊗ Chalk → y , we use the fact that Chalk is a coproduct and that ⊗ distributes over coproduct to write You ⊗ Chalk as a coproduct itself:

```
You ⊗ Chalk  y ⊗ { out } y + y ⊗ { in } y .
```

Then by the universal property of coproducts, to define , it suffices to define two lenses

```
: y ⊗ { out } y → y and : y ⊗ { in } y → y .
```

The lens , corresponding to when the chalk is in your possession, is easy to describe: it can be identified with a function → , and we take it to be the obvious function sending your height and pressure to the chalk and the chalk's height to you; see Exercise 4.53. But , corresponding to when the chalk is out of your possession, is more semantically interesting: it can be identified with a function → given by

![[Polynomial Functors_ A Mathematical Theory of Interaction.assets/formula-0243.png]]

In words, this says that if you and the chalk are at different heights, then regardless of your pressure, the chalk remains unpressed; but if you are at the same height as the chalk, the chalk receives your pressure.

Now that you and the chalk are sectioned off together by , we are ready to add some dynamics. Your dynamics can be whatever you want, so let us focus on giving dynamics to the chalk (you will be able to give yourself dynamics in Exercise 4.53). The chalk's state is comprised of its height and whether or not it is in your possession, so we give a dynamical system with state-set B { out , in } × and interface Chalk: that is, a lens

![[Polynomial Functors_ A Mathematical Theory of Interaction.assets/formula-0244.png]]

On positions, the chalk returns its height and whether it is in your possession directly: the on-positions function is the identity. On directions, we have two cases. If the chalk is out of your possession, it falls down unless you catch it, making it pressed so that it becomes in your possession and retains its current height. So we can express the on-directions function of (4.52) at ( out , ℎ Chalk ) as

unpressed ↦→ ( out , down ) ; pressed ↦→ ( in , ℎ Chalk ) .

On the other hand, if the chalk is in your possession, it takes whatever height it receives from you, remaining in your possession if pressed but coming out of your possession if unpressed . So the on-directions function of (4.52) at ( in , ℎ Chalk ) is given by

![[Polynomial Functors_ A Mathematical Theory of Interaction.assets/formula-0245.png]]

![[Polynomial Functors_ A Mathematical Theory of Interaction.assets/formula-0246.png]]

We have thus defined an interaction pattern that allows one system to engage with or disengage from another system and control the behavior of the other system only when the two are engaged.

## 4.4.3 Wiring diagrams as interaction patterns

A wiring diagram is a graphical depiction of interactions between systems. Wiring diagrams depict systems as boxes, showing how they send signals to each other through the wires between them, as well as how multiple systems can combine to form a larger system whenever smaller boxes are nested within a larger box.

Formally, and more precisely, we can think of each box in a wiring diagram as an interface given by some monomial. The box itself is not a dynamical system, but it becomes a dynamical system once we equip it with a lens from a state system to the interface the box represents. Then the entire wiring diagram-specifying how these boxes nest within a larger box-is just an interaction pattern between the corresponding interfaces, with the larger box playing the role of the wrapper interface.

Once every nested box is equipped with a lens from a state system, we obtain a dynamical system whose interface is the larger box.

In the examples to come, we follow the convention that the signals emitted by a box, i.e. positions returned by the corresponding interface, travel along wires out of the right side of that box; while the signals received, i.e. directions observed by the corresponding interface, by a box travel along wires into the left side of that box. A wire may optionally be labeled by the name of the set of elements that may travel as signals along that wire.

Example 4.60 . Here is a simple wiring diagram.

![[Polynomial Functors_ A Mathematical Theory of Interaction.assets/figure-0103.png]]

The Plant is receiving information from the world outside the System along the wire labeled as well as from the Controller along the wire labeled . It is also producing information for the outside world along the wire labeled which is also being monitored by the Controller .

There are three boxes shown in (4.61): the Controller , the Plant , and the System . Each has a fixed set of positions corresponding to the wire(s) connected to its right and a fi xed set of directions corresponding to the wire(s) connected to its left, so we can consider each box as a monomial interface, as follows:

![[Polynomial Functors_ A Mathematical Theory of Interaction.assets/formula-0248.png]]

Note that in the case of the Plant , two wires labeled and enter the box from the left, so we take their cartesian product to be the direction-set of the Plant .

The wiring diagram itself is a wrapper

## : Controller ⊗ Plant → System ,

specifying an interaction pattern between the Controller and the Plant with the System as the wrapper interface. Concretely, is a lens → y y that prescribes how wires feed positions to directions. As a lens between monomials, consists of an onpositions function → and an on-directions map → .

The wiring diagram is a picture that tells us what on-positions function and on-directions map to use. In particular, the onpositions function sends positions of the inner interfaces to positions of the outer interface, so it is depicted by how the wires coming from the right sides of the inner boxes connect to the right side of the outer box. Given inner boxes that return positions in and , the outer box must return a position in . Here the wire labeled is not connected to the outer box, but the wire labeled is, so the on-positions function → sends ( , ) ↦→ .

Meanwhile, the on-directions function sends positions of the inner interfaces and directions of the outer interface to directions of the inner interfaces, so it is depicted by how the wires coming from both the right sides of the inner boxes and the left side of the outer box connect to the left sides of the inner boxes. Given inner boxes that return positions in and and an outer box that receives directions in , the inner boxes must receive directions in and . Again, we can read the on-directions map → off the wiring diagram: it sends ( , , ) ↦→ ( , , ) .

Note that neither the wiring diagram nor any of the boxes within it represent dynamical systems on their own. Rather, each box is a monomial that could be the interface of a dynamical system. When we assign to a box a dynamical system having that box as its interface, we say that we give dynamics to the box. So the entire wiring diagram is a wrapper that tells us how, once we give dynamics for each inner box,

![[Polynomial Functors_ A Mathematical Theory of Interaction.assets/formula-0249.png]]

we have given dynamics for the entire outer box:

![[Polynomial Functors_ A Mathematical Theory of Interaction.assets/formula-0250.png]]

Example 4.66 (Graphs as wiring diagrams and cellular automata) . Suppose we have a graph = ( ⇒ ) as in Definition 4.25 and a set ( ) associated with each vertex ∈ :

![[Polynomial Functors_ A Mathematical Theory of Interaction.assets/formula-0251.png]]

We can think of as an alternative representation of a specific kind of wiring diagram, one in which each inner box has exactly one position wire coming out and the outer box is closed (i.e. no wires in or out, representing the interface y with exactly 1 position and 1 direction). The vertices ∈ are the inner boxes, the set ( ) is the position-set associated with the wire coming out of , and each edge is a wire connecting the position wire of its target ( ) to a direction wire of its source ( ) . An edge from a vertex 0 to a vertex 1 indicates that the directions received by 0 depend on the positions returned by 1 . 3

In other words, we can associate each vertex ∈ with the monomial

![[Polynomial Functors_ A Mathematical Theory of Interaction.assets/formula-0252.png]]

specifying its positions and directions, where B -1 ( ) ⊆ denotes the set of edges emanating from . The graph then determines a section

![[Polynomial Functors_ A Mathematical Theory of Interaction.assets/formula-0253.png]]

![[Polynomial Functors_ A Mathematical Theory of Interaction.assets/formula-0254.png]]

that sends each dependent function : ( ∈ ) → ( ) to the dependent function ( ∈ ) → ( ( )) sending ↦→ ( ( )) . In other

given by a function words, given the -position ( ) ∈ ( ) returned by each vertex ∈ , we know for each edge ∈ that the direction that its source vertex ( ) receives is the position ( ( )) returned by its target vertex ( ) .

So once we give dynamics to each , namely by specifying a dynamical system y → with positions in ( ) and directions Î in ( ( )) ∈ , we will obtain a closed dynamical system that updates the state of each vertex according to the information that they observe from each other along their edges.

Effectively, by interpreting a graph as a wiring diagram and giving each vertex dynamics, we have created what is known as a cellular automaton -a network of vertices (or cells ), each with an internal state, in which each vertex ∈ broadcasts a signal (i.e. returns a position) according to its current state, then updates its state according to the signals broadcasted by its neighbors in ( ) (i.e. positions returned by its neighbors it receives as directions).

For example, many cellular automata have their cells on a 2dimensional integer lattice. The corresponding graph has vertices B Z × Z and edges given by

![[Polynomial Functors_ A Mathematical Theory of Interaction.assets/formula-0255.png]]

with (( , ) , ( , )) = ( , ) and (( , ) , ( , )) = ( + , + ) , so that the neighbors of each vertex are the eight vertices that surround it.

Exercise 4.67 (Conway's Game of Life) . Conway's Game of Life is a cellular automaton taking place on a 2-dimensional integer lattice as follows. Each lattice point is either live or dead , and each point observes its eight neighbors to which it is horizontally, vertically, or diagonally adjacent. The following occurs at every time step:

- Any live point with 2 or 3 live neighbors remains live.
- Any dead point with 3 live neighbors becomes live.

3 We could swap the roles of the sources and targets, so that edges point in the direction of data fl ow rather than data dependencies; the choice is arbitrary.

- All other points either become or remain dead.

We can use Example 4.66 to model Conway's Game of Life as a closed dynamical system.

## 4.4.4 More examples of general interaction

While wiring diagrams are a handy visualization tool for certain simple interaction patterns, there are more general interaction patterns that cannot be captured by such a static diagram. For example, here we generalize our previous cellular automata example.

Example 4.68 (Generalized cellular automata: voting on who your neighbors are) . Recall from Example 4.66 how we constructed a cellular automaton on a graph = ( ⇒ ) . For each ∈ , the graph specifies the set ( ) B ( ) of target vertices of edges emanating from . These vertices are the neighbors of , or the vertices that can 'observe.' We call the function : → 2 from each vertex to the set of its neighbors the neighbor function . For simplicitly, we let each vertex store and return one of two states, so B ( ) B 2 .

Now consider only the vertices of our graph and forget the edges. Suppose we are then given a function : → N that we can think of as specifying the number ( ) of neighbors each ∈ could potentially have. Let n ( ) B { 1 , 2 , . . . , ( )} . Then the monomial each vertex represents is

![[Polynomial Functors_ A Mathematical Theory of Interaction.assets/formula-0256.png]]

with its own possible states as positions and its potential neighbors' possible states as directions.

Say that a neighbor function : → 2 respects if we have an isomorphism ( )  n ( ) for each ∈ . Now suppose we have a function ′ → ( ) -: 2 2 that sends each set of vertices ∈ 2 to a neighbor function ′ : → 2 that respects . In other words, each possible state configuration of all the vertices in determines a neighbor function ′ . In t case of Example 4.66, he when we had a graph, it told us what the neighbor function should always be. Now we could imagine all the vertices returning their states to vote, via ′ , on what neighbor function to use to determine which vertices are observing which others.

We can put this all together by providing a section for all the vertices,

![[Polynomial Functors_ A Mathematical Theory of Interaction.assets/formula-0257.png]]

Í Such a section is equivalent to a function : n 2 → 2 ( ) ∈ that sends each possible state configuration ∈ 2 of all the vertices in Í to a function ( ) : ) ∈ n ( → 2 specifying the states every vertex observes. But we already have a neighbor function assigned to that respects n , namely ′ : we have ′ ( )  n ( ) for all ∈ . So Í we can think of ( ) equivalently as a function ( ) : ′ ( ) → ∈ 2 that says for each ∈ what signal in 2 it should receive from its neighbor ∈ ′ ( ) . We will let it receive the current state of that neighbor, as given by :

![[Polynomial Functors_ A Mathematical Theory of Interaction.assets/formula-0258.png]]

We have accomplished our goal: the vertices 'vote' on how they should be connected in that their states together determine the neighbor function. We do not mean to imply that this vote needs to be democratic or fair in any way: it is an arbitrary function -′ : 2 → ( 2 ) . For instance, the state of a given vertex 0 ∈ may completely determine the neighbor function → 2 ; this would be expressed by saying that -′ factors as 2 → 2 { 0 }  2 → ( 2 ) , where the left function is the evaluation map at 0 ∈ .

Here are some more examples of dependent dynamical systems with interaction patterns in which the way the constituent components are wired together may change.

Example 4.70 . In the picture below, forces are applied to the connected boxes on the left; we would like to model how too much force could cause the connection between the boxes to sever, as depicted on the right.

![[Polynomial Functors_ A Mathematical Theory of Interaction.assets/figure-0107.png]]

We will imagine the dependent dynamical systems 1 : y → 1 and 2 : y → 2 as initially connected in space. They experience forces from the outside world, and-for as long as they are connected-they experience forces from each other. More precisely, their interfaces are given by

![[Polynomial Functors_ A Mathematical Theory of Interaction.assets/formula-0259.png]]

where is our set of forces . We need to be able to add and compare forces, so we need to be an ordered monoid; let us say B N for simplicity. Here each interface has two kinds of positions it can return: either a force ∈ that will be applied to the other interface (i.e. sent to the other interface as a direction) or snapped , indicating that the interfaces are no longer connected. The interface always receives a force from the outside world as part of its direction, but when the position of the interface is not snapped , it receives a force from the other interface as part of its direction as well. So its direction set is × at positions in , when the two interfaces are connected; and just at the position snapped , when the two interfaces are not. We define the wrapper interface to be

![[Polynomial Functors_ A Mathematical Theory of Interaction.assets/formula-0260.png]]

it can return only 1 position, while its directions are ordered pairs of forces ( ) , indicating the two external forces acting on the composite system.

Though the systems 1 and 2 may be initially connected, if the forces on either one surpass a threshold, that system stops communicating with the other system. The connection is broken and neither system ever receives forces from the other again. To implement this explicitly, we need to define an interaction pattern : 1 ⊗ 2 → that wraps around 1 and 2 . That is, we need to give a lens

![[Polynomial Functors_ A Mathematical Theory of Interaction.assets/formula-0261.png]]

Distributing and leveraging coproducts as usual, we fi nd that it suffices to give four lenses:

![[Polynomial Functors_ A Mathematical Theory of Interaction.assets/formula-0262.png]]

The lenses 12 and 21 will not actually occur in our dynamics (when one interface returns snapped , both should), so we take them to be arbitrary. We take the lens 22 to be the obvious isomorphism, passing the external forces to the two internal interfaces. Finally, the lens 11 is equivalent to a function ( × )( × ) → ( × )( × ) which, taking care to remember what each refers to, we find should send (( 1 , 2 ) , ( , )) ↦→ (( , 2 ) , ( 1 , )) . While the multiple 's may be a little hard to keep track of, what this map says is that if 1 returns the force 1 on 2 as output and 2 returns the force 2 on 1 as output, then 1 receives the force 2 from the right as input and 2 receives the force 1 from the left as input; and in the meantime the left external force is given to 1 on the left, while the right external force is given to 2 on the right.

Now that we have the interfaces wrapped together, it remains to specify each dynamical system. The state-sets for the two systems will be the same, namely B +{ snapped } : each system is either applying a force to the other system or not. The dynamical systems themselves will be the same as well, up to a symmetry swapping left and right; we will define only the left system. It is given by a lens

![[Polynomial Functors_ A Mathematical Theory of Interaction.assets/formula-0263.png]]

which we write as the sum of two lenses

![[Polynomial Functors_ A Mathematical Theory of Interaction.assets/formula-0264.png]]

Both lenses are identities on positions, directly returning their current states. The second lens corresponds to when the connection is broken, after which the connection should remain broken: so its ondirections function is constant, sending any direction to snapped . Meanwhile, the fi rst lens corresponds to the case where the systems are still connected; in this state, the system can receive a pair of forces as its direction and must update its state-either the force it applies or snapped -accordingly. We let the on-directions function ( × ) → + { snapped } send

![[Polynomial Functors_ A Mathematical Theory of Interaction.assets/formula-0265.png]]

Thus, when the sum of forces is above a certain threshold (arbitrarily chosen here to be 100), the internal state is updated to the snapped state; otherwise, the internal state is set to the external force applied to the system, which it is now ready to transfer to the other system.

Example 4.72 . Consider the case of a company that may change its supplier based on its internal state. The company returns two possible positions, corresponding to whether it wants to receive gizmos in from the fi rst supplier or widgets in from the second:

![[Polynomial Functors_ A Mathematical Theory of Interaction.assets/figure-0108.png]]

So the company has interface { 1 } y + { 2 } y , the fi rst supplier has interface y , and the second supplier has interface y . Then a section for the company and the suppliers is a lens

![[Polynomial Functors_ A Mathematical Theory of Interaction.assets/formula-0266.png]]

corresponding to a pair of functions { 1 } ×  → and { 2 } ×  → given by canonical projections. In other words, the company's position determines its supplier and what it receives.

Example 4.73 . When someone assembles a machine, their own positions dictate the interaction pattern of the machine's components.

We can easily generalize Example 4.73. Indeed, we will see in the next section that there is an interface [ 1 ⊗ · · · ⊗ , ] that represents all the interaction patterns between 1 , . . . , with wrapper interface , and that wrapping it around can be interpreted as a larger interaction pattern with wrapper interface :

![[Polynomial Functors_ A Mathematical Theory of Interaction.assets/formula-0270.png]]

In other words, if the positions returns is deciding the interaction pattern between 1 , . . . , with wrapper interface , and the directions receives is from the subsequent behavior of that interaction pattern itself, then this is equivalent to an interaction pattern with wrapper interface that is part of alongside 1 , . . . , .

What it also means is that a dynamical system with interface [ 1 ⊗ · · · ⊗ , ] is simply selecting interaction patterns 1 ⊗·· · ⊗ → . Let us see how this works.

## 4.5 Closure of ⊗

The parallel monoidal product is closed-we have a closed monoidal structure on Poly -meaning that there is a closure operation, which we denote [-, -] : Poly op × Poly → Poly , such that there is an isomorphism

![[Polynomial Functors_ A Mathematical Theory of Interaction.assets/formula-0271.png]]

natural in , , . The closure operation is defined on , as follows:

![[Polynomial Functors_ A Mathematical Theory of Interaction.assets/formula-0272.png]]

Here ◦ denotes standard functor composition; informally, ◦ ( [ ] y ) is the polynomial obtained by replacing each appearance of y in by [ ] y . Composition, together with the unit y , is in fact yet another monoidal structure, as we will cover in greater depth in Part II.

Before we prove that the isomorphism (4.74) holds naturally, let us investigate the properties of the closure operation, starting with some simple examples.

Example 4.94 (Chu &amp;) . Say we have 1 , 2 , 1 , 2 , ∈ Poly and lenses

![[Polynomial Functors_ A Mathematical Theory of Interaction.assets/formula-0282.png]]

One might call these ' -Chu spaces.' One operation you can do with these as Chu spaces is to return something denoted 1 &amp; 2 , or ' 1 with 2 ,' of the following type:

![[Polynomial Functors_ A Mathematical Theory of Interaction.assets/formula-0283.png]]

Suppose we are given a position in 1 and a position in 2 . Then given a position in either 1 or 2 , one evaluates either 1 or 2 respectively to get a position in ; given a direction there, one returns the corresponding direction in 1 or 2 respectively, as well as a direction in 1 × 2 which is either a direction in 1 or in 2 .

This sounds complicated, but it can be constructed formally via the monoidal closure. We use the closure to rewrite 1 and 2 :

![[Polynomial Functors_ A Mathematical Theory of Interaction.assets/formula-0284.png]]

Now we take their categorical product to obtain 1 × 2 : 1 × 2 → [ 1 , ] × [ 2 , ] . Then we apply Exercise 4.77 to find that [ 1 , ] × [ 2 , ]  [ 1 + 2 , ] , and finally leverage the monoidal closure again to obtain ( 1 × 2 ) ⊗ ( 1 + 2 ) → as desired.

## 4.6 Summary and further reading

In this chapter we explained how discrete dynamical systems can be expressed as certain lenses between polynomial functors. For example, a Moore machine has an input set , an output set , a set of states , a return function → , and an update function × → . All this is captured in a dependent lens

![[Polynomial Functors_ A Mathematical Theory of Interaction.assets/formula-0285.png]]

We discussed a generalization : y → , where the output is an arbitrary polynomial ∈ Poly . This is similar to the notion of interaction system defined by Hyvernat in [Hyv09]. We also talked about how to wire machines in parallel by using the parallel product ⊗ and how to add wrapper interfaces by composing with lenses → .

Throughout the chapter we gave quite a few different examples. For example, we discussed how every function → counts as a memoryless dynamical system. In fact, it was shown in [BPS19] that every dynamical system can be obtained by wiring together memoryless ones. We discussed examples such as fi le-readers, moving robots, colliding particles, companies that change their suppliers, materials that break when too much force is applied, etc.

For further reading on the mathematics of Moore machines, see [Con12]. For more on mode-dependent interaction, see [ST17]. For a similar and complementary categorical approach to dynamical systems, we recommend David Jaz Myers' Categorical Systems Theory book, found at http://davidjaz.com/Papers/DynamicalBook.pdf .

## Solution to Exercise 4.8.

At any time, the Moore machine in Example 4.7 is located at a point on the coordinate plane, say ( , ) ∈ R 2 . This location is its current state. When we ask the machine to return its position, it will tell us those coordinates, since the return function is the identity. Then if we give the machine a direction ( , ) for some distance ∈ [ 0 , 1 ] and angle ∈ [ 0 , 2 ) , the machine will move by that distance, at that angle counterclockwise from the positive -axis, from ( , ) to

![[Polynomial Functors_ A Mathematical Theory of Interaction.assets/formula-0289.png]]

(here we treat R 2 as a vector space, so that ( cos , sin ) is a vector of length at the angle ).

## Solution to Exercise 4.11.

For each of the following constructs, we fi nd , ∈ Set such that the construct can be identified with a lens : y → y , i.e. a return function 1 : → and an update function ♯ : × → .

1. Given a discrete dynamical system with states and transition funtion : → , we can set B B 1 . Then 1 : → 1 is unique, while ♯ : × 1 → is given by × 1  - → . The corresponding Moore machine can only be fed one direction (you could think of that direction as a button that simply says 'advance to the next state') and can only return one position (which tells us no information). So it is just a set of states and a deterministic way to move from state to state. We could have also set B 0 and B , so that 1 B and ♯ : × 0 → is unique, but this formulation is somewhat less satisfying: this is a Moore machine that never moves between its states, effectively functioning as a lookup table between whatever state the machine happens to be in and its position, which also happens to refer to some state.
2. Given a magma consisting of a set and a function : × → , we can set B and B 1 . Then 1 : → 1 is unique, while ♯ : × → is equal to .

The corresponding Moore machine always returns the same position. It uses the binary operation to combine the current state with a given direction-which also refers to a state-to obtain the new state.

Alternatively, we could have set the update function to be with its inputs swapped. The difference here is that the new state is given by applying with the direction on the left and the current state on the right, rather than the other way around. If is noncommutative, this would yield a different Moore machine.

We could have also set B 0 and B , so that ♯ : × 0 → is unique, while currying gives 1 , so that 1 is the function → given by ′ ↦→ ( , ′ ) . Alternatively, 1 could be the function ′ ↦→ ( ′ , ) . Either way, this is again a Moore machine that never moves between its states, functioning as a lookup table between the machine's current state and the function partially applied to that state on one side or the other.

3. Given a set and a subset ′ ⊆ , we can set B 0 and B 2 . Then ♯ : × 0 → is unique, while we define 1 : → 2 by

![[Polynomial Functors_ A Mathematical Theory of Interaction.assets/formula-0290.png]]

Then ′ can be recovered from 1 as its fi ber over 1. The corresponing Moore machine never moves between its states, but returns one of two positions indicating whether or not the current state is in the subset ′ .

## Solution to Exercise 4.34.

Given a dynamical system : y → , we seek a new dynamical system ′ : y → ′ with an additional direction that does not change the state. We can think of this as having two different interfaces acting on the same system: the original interface of and a new interface with only one possible direction that does not change the state. This latter interface also need not distinguish between its positions; it should have a single position that provides no additional information. So the second interface we want acting on y is y .

If y were the only interface acting on the state system, we would have a Moore machine : y → y whose return function is the unique function → 1 and whose update function should be the identity function on , since the direction never changes the state. Then ′ is the product of the two interfaces and , while ′ : y y → ′ is the unique Í lens induced 1 by : y → and : y → y . In particular, ′  y  [ ]+ ∈ ( 1 ) y , and if we let ∗ denote the additional direction not in at each position of y , we can

## Solution to Exercise 4.48.

We define a new tracker ′ y ′ → N y 2 based on the one from Example 4.47 to watch for when the paddle switches sides once, at which point the tracker should increase its location by one, and watch for when the paddle switches sides twice in a row, at which point the tracker should increase its location by two. To do this, we need the tracker to remember not just the current side the paddle is on, but the previous side the paddle was on as well. The tracker should still remember the current location. Thus the states of the tracker are given by B 2 × 2 × N , storing the previous side the paddle was on, the current side the paddle is on, and the current location. The on-positions function of the tracker is the canonical projection 2 × 2 × N → N that returns the current location; then at each ( , ′ , ) ∈ 2 × 2 × N , the on-directions function of the tracker 2 → 2 × 2 × N sends

![[Polynomial Functors_ A Mathematical Theory of Interaction.assets/formula-0310.png]]

 storing both the last side the paddle was on and the new side the paddle is on as well as moving the machine forward one unit if the paddle switches after not switching and two units if the paddle switches after just switching.

We should therefore define the function → to be the isomorphism that sends ( ℎ You , You , ℎ Chalk ) ↦→ ( ℎ Chalk , ℎ You , You ) .

2. If you always cycle through three possible actions-reaching down and grabbing the chalk so that it is pressed , moving your hand up while keeping it pressed to lift the chalk, and dropping the chalk by leaving it unpressed while your hand is still up -you only need 3 possible states. So we can provide your dynamics using a lens 3 y 3 → You = y . The return function 3 → indicates what happens at each state, sending 1 ↦→ ( down , pressed ) , 2 ↦→ ( up , pressed ) , and 3 ↦→ ( up , unpressed ) . Meanwhile the update function 3 → 3 always changes the state to the next one, regardless of direction: it ignores the coordinate and sends 1 ↦→ 2, 2 ↦→ 3, and 3 ↦→ 1.

## 5. More categorical properties of polynomials

The category Poly has very useful formal properties, including many adjunctions with Set , factorization systems, cartesian closure, completion under colimits and limits, and so on. We detail some of these properties here. Most of the following material is not necessary for the development of our main story, but we collect it here for reference. The reader may skip directly to Part II if so inclined and check back here when needed; or they may skim the chapter to see how well-behaved and versatile Poly is.

## 5.1 Special polynomials and adjunctions

In Section 2.2, we identified four special classes of polynomials:

- a) constant polynomials for ∈ Set ;
- b) linear polynomials y for ∈ Set ;
- c) representable polynomials y for ∈ Set ; and
- d) monomials y for , ∈ Set .

The fi rst two classes, constant and linear polynomials, are interesting because they may each be viewed as a copy of Set inside Poly , as we will see in Propositions 5.2 and 5.3. The third class may be viewed as a copy of Set op inside Poly via the Yoneda embedding, as we saw in Exercise 1.12. Finally, the fourth may be viewed as a copy of the category of bimorphic lenses in Poly , as we saw in Example 3.41.

Proposition 5.2. There is a fully faithful functor Set → Poly sending sets to constants: ↦→ 0 y = .

Proof. A lens : y 0 → y 0 consists of a function 1 : → and, for each ∈ , a function 0 → 0 . There is exactly one function 0 → 0 , so can be identified with just a function between sets → ; in particular Set ( , )  Poly ( y 0 , y 0 ) . This identification respects identities and composition, so it defines a fully faithful functor. □

Proposition 5.3. There is a fully faithful functor Set → Poly sending ↦→ y .

Proof. A lens : y → y consists of a function 1 : → and, for each ∈ , a function 1 → 1 . There is exactly one function 1 → 1 , so can be identified with just a function between sets → ; in particular Set ( , )  Poly ( y , y ) . This identification respects identities and composition, so it defines a fully faithful functor. □
