We are often interested in whether epimorphisms and monomorphisms form what is called a factorization system in a given category, which we define here.

Definition 5.26 (Factorization system) . Given a category C and two classes of morphisms and in C , we say that ( , ) is a

## factorization system of C if:

1. every morphism in C factors uniquely (up to unique isomorphism) as a morphism ∈ composed with a morphism ∈ , so that = ;
2. # 2. and each contain every isomorphism; and
3. and are each closed under
4. composition.

If is the class of epimorphisms and is the class of monomorphisms (in which case conditions 2 and 3 are automatically satisfied), we say that C has epi-mono factorization .

Example 5.27 (Epi-mono factorization in Set ) . The category Set has epi-mono factorization: a function : → can be uniquely factored into an epimorphism (surjection) followed by a monomorphism (injection) , as follows. The epimorphism : → ( ) is given by restricting the codomain of to its image (also known as corestricting ), so sends ↦→ ( ) for all ∈ . The monomorphism : ( ) → is then given by including the image into the codomain, so sends ↦→ for all ∈ ( ) ⊆ .

## 5.4 Limits and colimits of polynomials

We have already seen that Poly has all coproducts (Proposition 3.3) and products (Proposition 3.56). We will now see that Poly has all small limits and colimits.

Theorem 5.34. The category Poly has all small limits.

Proof. A category has all small limits if and only if it has products and equalizers, so by Proposition 3.56, it suffices to show that Poly has equalizers.

We claim that equalizers in Poly are equalizers on positions and coequalizers on directions. More precisely, given lenses , : ⇒ , we construct their equalizer ′ ∈ Poly as follows. 2 We define its position-set ′ ( 1 ) to be the equalizer of 1 , 1 : ( 1 ) ⇒ ( 1 ) in Set ; that is,

2 Technically, a '(co)equalizer' is an object equipped with a morphism, but we will use the term to refer to either just the object or just the morphism when the context is clear.

Then for each ∈ ′ ( 1 ) , we define the direction-set ′ [ ] to be the co-♯ equalizer of ♯ , : [ 1 ] ⇒ [ ] . In this way, we obtain a polynomial ′ that comes equipped with a lens : ′ → . One can check that ′ together with satisfies the universal property of the equalizer of and ; see Exercise 5.35. □

Example 5.36 (Computing general limits in Poly ) . The proof of Theorem 5.34 justifies the following mnemonic for limits in Poly :

The positions of the limit are the limit of the positions. The directions of the limit are the colimit of the directions.

We can make this precise as follows: the limit of a functor -: J → Poly is the polynomial whose position-set is

![[Polynomial Functors_ A Mathematical Theory of Interaction.assets/formula-0387.png]]

equipped with a canonical projection to each ( 1 ) , and whose direction-set for each position is

We have seen (5.37) and (5.38) to be true for products: the position-set of the product is just the product of the original positionsets, while the direction-set at a tuple of the original positions is just the coproduct of the direction-sets at every position in the tuple. We have also just shown (5.37) and (5.38) to be true for equalizers in the proof of Theorem 5.34. It follows from the construction of any limit as an equalizer of products that it is true for arbitrary limits.

Example 5.46 . Given a diagram in Poly , one could either take its (co)limit as a diagram of polynomial functors (i.e. its (co)limit in Poly ) or its (co)limit simply as a diagram of functors (i.e. its (co)limit in Set Set ). We saw in Exercise 5.43 that in the case of limits, these yield the same result; so, too, in the case of coproducts, per Proposition 3.3.

But in the case of general colimits, there are diagrams that yield different results: after all, by the co-Yoneda lemma, every functor Set → Set -even those that are not polynomials-can be written as the colimit of representable functors in Set Set , yet the colimit of the same representables in Poly can only be another polynomial.

## 5.5 Vertical-cartesian factorization of lenses

Aside from epi-mono factorization, there is another useful factorization system on Poly .

Definition 5.52 (Vertical and cartesian lenses) . Let : → be a lens. It is called vertical if the on-positions function 1 : ( 1 ) → ( 1 ) is an isomorphism. It is called cartesian if, for each ∈ ( 1 ) , the on-directions function ♯ : [ 1 ] → [ ] is an isomorphism.

Proposition 5.53. Vertical and cartesian lenses form a factorization system of Poly .

Proof. It follows from the definitions that isomorphisms are both vertical and cartesian and that vertical and cartesian lenses are each closed under composition. It remains to show that every lens : → in Poly can be factored uniquely (up to unique isomorphism) as a vertical lens composed with a cartesian lens.

If : → is a vertical lens and : → is cartesian, then # = if and only if ♯ ♯ ♯ 1 ∈ # 1 = 1 and # ( ) = for all 1 . Exercise 5.60 . Is the pushout of a cartesian lens always cartesian?

Why do we use the word cartesian to describe cartesian lenses ? It turns out that, as natural transformations, cartesian morphisms are precisely what are known as cartesian natural transformations.

Definition 5.61 (Cartesian natural transformation) . A cartesian natural transformation is a natural transformation whose naturality squares are all pullbacks. That is, given categories C , D , functors , : C ⇒ D , and a natural transformation : → , we say that is cartesian if for all morphisms ℎ : ′ → in C , the induced naturality square is a pullback square in D .

![[Polynomial Functors_ A Mathematical Theory of Interaction.assets/figure-0126.png]]

There is a simpler characterization of cartesian natural transformations when C has a terminal object 1 by the pasting lemma, a standard result about pullbacks that we review.

Lemma 5.62 (Pasting lemma) . Given a commutative diagram

![[Polynomial Functors_ A Mathematical Theory of Interaction.assets/figure-0127.png]]

in which the right square is a pullback, the left square is a pullback if and only if the composite rectangle is a pullback.

Lemma 5.63. Given categories C , D , functors , : C ⇒ D , and a natural transformation : → , if C has a terminal object 1 , then is cartesian if and only if for all ∈ C , the naturality square induced by the unique morphism ! : → 1 is a pullback in D :

![[Polynomial Functors_ A Mathematical Theory of Interaction.assets/figure-0128.png]]

Proof. This follows from Lemma 5.62; see the following exercise.

Definition 5.67 ((Op)cartesian morphisms, (op)fibrations, and bifibrations) . Let Φ : A → B be a functor, let : → be a morphism in B , and let ∈ A be such that Φ ( ) = . ## 5.7 Cartesian lenses are exponentiable

We conclude this chapter with a discussion of what are called exponentiable morphisms in Poly . To introduce these, we fi rst need the following definition.

Definition 5.79 ((Co)slice category) . Given an object in a category C , the slice (resp. coslice ) category of C over (resp. under ) , denoted C / (resp. / C ), is the category whose objects are morphisms in C with codomain (resp. domain ) and whose morphisms are commutative triangles in C . By convention, we often refer to an object in C / (resp. / C ) only by its domain (resp. codomain) in C when the morphism is implied.

## 5.8 Summary and further reading

In this chapter, we discussed several categorical properties of Poly : it has various adjunctions with Set and Set op , including a ∗ -bifibration to Set ; it is cartesian closed; it has limits and colimits; and it has both an epi-mono factorization system and a vertical-cartesian factorization system.

The principal monomial functor ↦→ ( 1 ) y Γ discussed in Corollary 5.15 is in fact distributive monoidal and comes up in work on entropy [Spi22] and on noncooperative strategic games [Cap22].

## 5.9 Exercise solutions

Solution to Exercise 5.1.

Here , , , ∈ Set .

1. We determine whether each class of polynomials is closed under coproducts.
- a) Constants are closed under coproducts: given constants , , their coproduct + is also a constant.
- b) Linear polynomials are closed under coproducts: given linear polynomials y , y , their coproduct y + y  ( + ) y is also linear.
- c) Representables are not closed under coproducts: for example, y is a representable, but the coproduct of y with itself, 2 y , is not.
- d) Monomials are not closed under addition: for example, y and 2 y 3 are monomials, but their coproduct y + 2 y 3 is not.
2. We determine whether each class of polynomials is closed under products.
- a) Constants are closed under products: given constants , , their product is also a constant.
- b) Linear polynomials are not closed under products: for example, y and 2 y are linear polynomials, but their product 2 y 2 is not.
- c) Representables are closed under products: given representables y , y , their product + y is also a representable.

- d) Monomials are closed under products: given monomials y , y , their product + y is also a monomial.
3. We determine whether each class of polynomials is closed under parallel products.
- a) Constants are closed under parallel products: given constants , , their parallel product is also a constant.
- b) Linear polynomials are closed under parallel products: given linear polynomials y , y , their parallel product y is also linear.
- c) Representables are closed under parallel products: given representables y , y , their parallel product y is also a representable.
- d) Monomials are closed under parallel products: given monomials y , y , their parallel product y is also a monomial.
4. We determine whether each class of polynomials is closed under composition.
- a) Constants are closed under composition: given constants , , their composite ◦  is also a constant.
- b) Linear polynomials are closed under composition: given linear polynomials y , y , their composite y ◦ y  ( y )  y is also linear.
- c) Representables are closed under composition: given representables y , y , their composite y ◦ y  ( y )  y is also a representable.
- d) Monomials are closed under composition: given monomials y , y , their composite ◦  ( )  y y y y is also a monomial.

## Solution to Exercise 5.20.

We 12 are given a monomorphism : 12 y → N y N from Example 5.19. Let N : N y → be a dynamical system with return function 1 : N → ( 1 ) and update functions ♯ : [ 1 ] → N for each state ∈ N . Then the new composite dynamical system ℎ B # has a return function ℎ 1 : 12 → ( 1 ) which sends each state ∈ 12 to the position ℎ 1 = 1 1 = 1 , the same position that the original system returns in the state ∈ N . Meanwhile, the update function for each state ∈ 12 is a function ℎ ♯ : [ 1 ] → ∈ 12 which, given a direction [ ] 1 , updates the state from to ℎ ♯ = ♯ ♯ = ♯ mod 12, which is where the original system would have taken 1 the same state, but reduced modulo 12. In other words, the new system behaves like the old system but with only the states in 12 ⊆ N retained, and on any input that would have caused the old system to move to a state outside of 12 , the new system moves to the equivalent state (modulo 12) within 12 instead.

## Solution to Exercise 5.64.

One direction is immediate, so assume that the naturality squares of induced by unique morphisms to 1 are pullbacks; it suffices to show that all naturality squares of are pullbacks. Given a morphism ℎ : ′ → in C , consider the commutative diagram consisting of the naturality squares for induced by ℎ and by the unique morphism ! : → 1 :

we know that the square on the right is a pullback square. By functoriality, the pairs of arrows on the top and bottom compose to ( ℎ # ! ) and ( ℎ # ! ) , respectively; then by the universal property of the terminal object, ℎ # ! is the unique arrow ′ → 1 . It follows that the composite rectangle is the naturality square of induced by the unique arrow ′ → 1 , so it is a pullback as well. Hence the square on the left is a pullback by Lemma 5.62.

## Part II

## A different category of categories

## 6. The composition product

We have seen that the category Poly of polynomial functors has plenty of interoperating mathematical structure. Further, it provides an expressive way to model dynamical systems that can change their interfaces and wiring patterns based on their internal states.

But we touched upon one aspect of the theory only briefly. That aspect is quite simple to state, yet it has profound consequences. Namely, polynomials can be composed:

![[Polynomial Functors_ A Mathematical Theory of Interaction.assets/formula-0460.png]]

In other words, ( y + 1 ) is substituted for the variable 2 y in y .

It turns out that this operation, which we will soon see is a monoidal product, has much to do with the concept of time . There is a sensemade precise in Proposition 6.2-in which the polynomial ◦ represents 'starting at a -position , choosing a [ ] -direction, landing at a -position , and choosing a [ ] -direction.' This is exactly what we need to run through multiple steps of a dynamical system: the very thing we did not know how to do in Example 4.43. We will continue that story in Section 6.1.4.

Throughout the rest of this book, we will see that the composition product is surprisingly versatile, with many interesting properties. 1

## 6.1 Interpreting the composition product

There are many ways to define and interpret the composition product in Poly , and we will introduce and explore them throughout this section.

1 Some authors refer to ⊳ as the substitution product, rather than the composition product. We elect to use the composition product terminology because it provides a good noun form 'the composite' for ⊳ , whereas 'the substitute' is misleading.

## 6.1.1 Composite functors

We begin with the definition of the composition product in terms of polynomials as functors.

Definition 6.1 (Composition product) . Given polynomial functors , , we let ◦ denote their composition product , or their composite as functors. That is, ◦ : Set → Set sends each set to the set ( ( )) .

Functor composition gives a monoidal structure on the category Set Set of functors Set → Set , but to check that the full subcategory Poly of Set Set inherits this monoidal structure, we need to verify that the composite of two functors in Poly is still a functor in Poly .

Proposition 6.2. Suppose , ∈ Poly are polynomial functors. Then their composite ◦ is again a polynomial functor, and we have the following isomorphism:

Corollary 6.4. The category Poly has a monoidal structure ( y , ◦) , where y is the identity functor and ◦ is given by composition.

Instead of the symbol ◦ , which may denote composition in any category, we use a special symbol for polynomial composition, namely

The symbol ⊳ resembles the composition symbol in that it is an open shape. But ⊳ highlights the asymmetry of composition, in contrast with the other monoidal structures on Poly we have encountered. Moreover, ⊳ evokes the trees that we will soon introduce as a way to depict polynomial composition.

For each ∈ N , we will also use ⊳ to denote the -fold composition product of , i.e. copies of all composed with each other. 2 In particular, ⊳ 0 = y and ⊳ 1 = .

Now that we know how ⊳ acts on the objects of Poly , we can examine its behavior on the morphisms. For any pair of natural transformations : → ′ and : → ′ between polynomial functors, their composite ⊳ : ⊳ → ′ ⊳ ′ is given by horizontal composition .

Definition 6.10 (Horizontal composition of natural transformations) . Let : → ′ and : → ′ be two natural transformations between (polynomial) functors , ′ , , ′ : Set → Set . Then the horizontal composite of and , denoted ⊳ , is the natural transformation ⊳ → ′ ⊳ ′ whose -component for each ∈ Set is the function

Remark 6.14 . So far, we have introduced two different notions of lens composition, so let us mitigate confusion by standardizing terminology. We reserve the term composite lens for lenses ℎ # : → obtained by composing a lens ℎ : → with a lens : → , according to the composition rule of the category Poly . This corresponds to vertical composition of natural transformations. This is also the kind of composition we mean whenever we use the verb compose , if the objects of that verb are lenses.

Meanwhile, we use the term composition product (of lenses) for lenses ⊳ : ⊳ → ′ ⊳ ′ obtained by applying the monoidal product functor ⊳ : Poly × Poly → Poly on the lenses : → ′ and : → ′ . This corresponds to horizontal composition of natural transformations. In this case, we use the verb phrase taking the monoidal product .

On the other hand, we use the terms composite and composition product interchangeably to refer to polynomials ⊳ , obtained by composing , ∈ Poly as functors or, equivalently, applying the monoidal product functor ⊳ on them-there is no risk of confusion here.

This is another reason we avoid the symbol ◦ , preferring to use # or juxtaposition for vertical composition and ⊳ for horizontal composition. Of course, if you are ever confused, you can always check whether the codomain of the fi rst lens matches up with the domain of the second: if they do not, we must be taking their monoidal product.

Composition products of polynomials and lenses will be crucial in what follows. But just as we prefer to think of polynomials in terms of their positions and directions rather than as functors, we seek a more concrete interpretation of the composition product in terms of positions and directions as well.

## 6.1.2 Composite positions and directions

Let us interpret our formula (6.6) for the composition product of two polynomials in terms of positions and directions. The position-set of ⊳ is

![[Polynomial Functors_ A Mathematical Theory of Interaction.assets/formula-0472.png]]

So we can identify a ( ⊳ ) -position with a -position along with a function : [ ] → ( 1 ) , i.e. a -position for each [ ] -direction .

Given such a ( ⊳ ) -position ( , ) , the direction-set of ⊳ at ( , ) is then

![[Polynomial Functors_ A Mathematical Theory of Interaction.assets/formula-0473.png]]

So a ( ⊳ )[( , )] -direction consists of a [ ] -direction and a [ ] -direction.

While this description completely characterizes ⊳ , it may appear rather opaque. Here is an alternate perspective that can provide more intuition for the composition product of polynomials.

Back in Section 1.2, we saw how to write the instructions for choosing an element of a sum or product of sets. For instance, given a polynomial and a set , the instructions for choosing an element of

![[Polynomial Functors_ A Mathematical Theory of Interaction.assets/formula-0474.png]]

would be written as follows.

To choose an element of ( ) :

1. choose an element ∈ ( 1 ) ;
2. for each element ∈ [ ] :
3. 2.1. choose an element of .

But say we had not picked a set yet and might replace with an arbitrary polynomial instead. We will replace 'an element of ' with a placeholder-the words 'a future'-indicating that we do not yet know what will go there. Furthermore, to highlight that these instructions are associated with some polynomial , we will use our familiar positions and directions terminology.

The instructions associated with a polynomial are:

1. choose a -position ;
2. for each [ ] -direction :
3. 2.1. choose a future.

If we think of polynomials in terms of their instructions, then (6.5) tells us that the composition product simply nests one set of instructions within another, as follows.

The instructions associated with a polynomial ⊳ are:

1. choose a -position ;
2. for each [ ] -direction :
3. 2.1. choose a -position ;
4. 2.2. for each [ ] -direction :
5. 2.2.1. choose a future.

Similarly, we could write down the instructions associated with any -fold composition product by nesting further. We might think of such instructions as specifying a lengthstrategy , in the sense of game theory, for choosing positions given any directions chosen by an opponent.

When we rewrite (6.5) as (6.6), we collapse the associated instructions into the following, highlighting the positions and directions of ⊳ .

The instructions associated with a polynomial ⊳ are:

1. choose a -position and, for each [ ] -direction , a -position ;
2. for each [ ] -direction and each [ ] -direction :
3. 2.1. choose a future.

We will see in Section 6.1.3 that these instructions have a natural interpretation when we depict these polynomials as corolla forests.

## 6.1.3 Composition product on corolla forests

It turns out that the forest of ⊳ is given by grafting -corollas onto the leaves of -corollas in every possible way. We will demonstrate this using an example.

Let B y 2 + y and B y 3 + 1 , whose corolla forests we draw as follows:

![[Polynomial Functors_ A Mathematical Theory of Interaction.assets/figure-0140.png]]

By (6.15), choosing a position of ⊳ amounts to fi rst choosing a -corolla , then choosing a -corolla for every [ ] -leaf. For now, we can disregard the leaves of these -corolla, as they do not play a part in the positions of ⊳ . So we may depict ( ⊳ )( 1 ) by grafting roots from the corolla forest of to leaves in the corolla forest of in every possible way, as follows:

![[Polynomial Functors_ A Mathematical Theory of Interaction.assets/figure-0141.png]]

Now fi x one of the positions of ⊳ drawn above: a -corolla and a -corolla grafted to every [ ] -leaf. By (6.16), a direction of ⊳ at that position consists of a [ ] -leaf and a second leaf emanating from the -corolla that has been grafted on to . In other words, in the following picture, where we have grafted not just roots but entire -corollas onto leaves in , the directions of ⊳ at the position corresponding to each tree are the rooted paths 3 of that tree of length 2 (we omit the labels):

![[Polynomial Functors_ A Mathematical Theory of Interaction.assets/figure-0142.png]]

(6.23)

Equivalently, we can think of the directions in the picture above as the leaves at the second level of each tree. So ⊳ has six positions: the fi rst has six directions; the second, third, and fi fth each have three directions; and the fourth and sixth have no directions. In total, we can read off that ⊳ is isomorphic to y 6 + 3 y 3 + 2 .

We put the ⊳ in scare quotes above (6.23) because, to be pedantic, the corolla forest of ⊳ should have leaves of height 1 instead of 2:

![[Polynomial Functors_ A Mathematical Theory of Interaction.assets/figure-0143.png]]

Usually, we will prefer the style of (6.23) rather than the more pedantic style of (6.24).

We have now seen how to draw a single polynomial as a corolla forest, with height-1 leaves as directions; as well as how to draw a 2-fold composite of polynomials as a forest of trees, with height-2 leaves as directions. We can verify the validity of these pictures by observing that drawing a corolla of or a tree of ⊳ is just a graphical way of following the instructions associated with the polynomial or ⊳ that we saw in Section 6.1.2, where the arrows-the top-level leaves-are where the 'futures' would go. Similarly, we could depict any -fold composite as a forest with heightleaves as directions. You will have an opportunity to try this in the following exercise.

## 6.1.4 Dynamical systems and the composition product

Back in Example 4.43, we asked how Poly could model running multiple steps of a dynamical system. The answer is that we can use the composition product.

Recall our definition of a (dependent) dynamical system : y → from Definition 4.18. More generally, we saw in Example 4.43 that we could replace the state system y with a monomial B y ′ , where ′ is another set, as long as there is a bijection : → ′ , which we can view as a certain section : y ′ → y .

Then the lens : → models a dynamical system as follows. Every state ∈ ( 1 ) = returns a position B 1 ∈ ( 1 ) , at which every direction yields an updated direction ′ B ♯ ∈ [ ] ∈ [ ] = ′ . Then to model a second step through the system, we identify the [ ] -direction ′ with a -position -1 ( ′ ) , plug this position back into 1 , and repeat the process.

But this is exactly what the composition product ⊳ : ⊳ → ⊳ does: by (6.19), its on-positions function sends ( 0 , -1 ) ∈ ( ⊳ )( 1 ) , comprised of an initial state 0 ∈ ( 1 ) and the function -1 : [ 0 ] = ′ → = ( 1 ) , to

![[Polynomial Functors_ A Mathematical Theory of Interaction.assets/formula-0479.png]]

4 When the name of an object is used in place of a morphism, we refer to the identity morphism on that object. So for instance, ⊳ is the composition product of with the identity lens on .

comprised of the initial position 0 B 1 ∈ ( 1 ) and a composite function

![[Polynomial Functors_ A Mathematical Theory of Interaction.assets/formula-0480.png]]

which uses the update function at 0 and the return function to tell us what the next position 1 will be for every possible direction 1 we could select. Then by (6.20), the on-directions function of ⊳ sends each direction ( 1 , 2 ) at the position (6.33), comprised of an initial direction 1 ∈ [ 0 ] and (setting 1 to be the function (6.34) applied to 1 ) a second direction 2 ∈ [ 1 ] , to the pair

![[Polynomial Functors_ A Mathematical Theory of Interaction.assets/formula-0481.png]]

comprised of directions in ′ that (under -1 ) correspond to the next state 1 upon selecting direction 1 at state 0 and the successive state 2 upon selecting direction 2 at 1 . In summary, at certain positions, ⊳ tells us how the dynamical system will behave when we step through it twice: starting from state 0 , returning position 0 , receiving direction 1 , updating its state to 1 , returning position 1 , receiving direction 2 , and preparing to update its state to 2 . Adding another layer, ⊳ ⊳ : ⊳ ⊳ → ⊳ ⊳ will tell us how the system behaves when we step through it three times; and in general, ⊳ : ⊳ → ⊳ will tell us how the system behaves when we step through it times.

Example 6.35 (Composition products of dynamical systems as trees) . Consider the dynamical system : y → with B y + 1 , corresponding to the halting deterministic state automaton (4.24) from Exercise 4.23, depicted again here for convenience:

![[Polynomial Functors_ A Mathematical Theory of Interaction.assets/figure-0151.png]]

Below, we draw the corolla pictures for y and for .

![[Polynomial Functors_ A Mathematical Theory of Interaction.assets/figure-0152.png]]

![[Polynomial Functors_ A Mathematical Theory of Interaction.assets/figure-0153.png]]

In the picture for y , the roots are the three states { ■ , ▲ , } appearing in the automaton, while the leaves of each corolla correspond to the three states as well, from left to right. In the picture for , there is one corolla whose two leaves correspond to the two arrows coming out of every state except the halting state, which is sent to the corolla with no leaves instead. So the lens : y → capturing the dynamics of the automaton can be drawn as follows:

![[Polynomial Functors_ A Mathematical Theory of Interaction.assets/figure-0154.png]]

The corolla picture tells us, for example, that from the ▲ state, we can take one of two directions: the solid blue direction, which leads us to the ■ state, or the dashed red direction, which leads us to the state. This describes the dynamics of the automaton one step away from the ▲ state.

But what if we want to understand the dynamics of two steps away from the ▲ state? Consider the following tree, corresponding to a position of the 2-fold composite y ⊳ y , where each height-1 vertex has been selected to match the leaf it is grafted to:

![[Polynomial Functors_ A Mathematical Theory of Interaction.assets/figure-0155.png]]

We can follow the steps from Example 6.30 to fi nd that the composition product of lenses ⊳ : y ⊳ y → ⊳ acts on this tree This is what we meant at the start of this chapter when we said that the composition product has to do with time . It takes a specification for how a state system and an interface can interact back-and-forth-or, indeed, for any interaction pattern between wrapper interfaces-and extends it to a multistep model ⊳ that simulates successive interaction cycles over time, accounting for all possible external directions that the interface could encounter. Alternatively, we can think of ⊳ as 'speeding up' the original dynamical system by a factor of , as it runs steps in one-as long as whatever is connected to its new interface ⊳ can keep up with its pace and feed it directions of at a time. The lens tells us how the machine can run, but it is ⊳ that makes the clock tick.

![[Polynomial Functors_ A Mathematical Theory of Interaction.assets/figure-0156.png]]

## Why this is not enough

There are several issues we must address, however, before we can provide a satisfying answer to all we asked for in Example 4.43. The fi rst is a communication issue: as you probably sensed, our set-theoretic notation for ⊳ is rather cumbersome, and that was just for = 2. We could depict the behavior of our composition products of dynamical systems more clearly using tree pictures (see Example 6.35), but even that becomes infeasible in greater generality. A concise visual representation of the back-and-forth interaction of lenses would help us reason about composition products more effectively.

The second issue is more technical: to ensure that ⊳ behaves the way we want, when we specify a position of its domain ⊳ , we must provide not only an initial state 0 but also the isomorphism -1 : ′ → to let the lens know which state in each [ 0 ] -direction in ′ should lead to. But there are many other positions ( 0 , ) of ⊳ that we could have specified, and each : ′ → associates [ 0 ] -directions to -positions in a different way: see the following example.

Example 6.37 (Composition products of dynamical systems can be misleading) . In Example 6.35, instead of (6.36), we could have picked the following tree, corresponding to a different (yet entirely valid) position of y ⊳ y :

![[Polynomial Functors_ A Mathematical Theory of Interaction.assets/figure-0157.png]]

The composition product ⊳ : ⊳ y y → ⊳ acts on this tree like so:

![[Polynomial Functors_ A Mathematical Theory of Interaction.assets/figure-0158.png]]

Now the picture tells us that, starting from the ▲ state, it is the solid blue arrow that will lead to a halting state, whereas we should somehow be able to follow the dashed red arrow twice!

Of course, this is nonsense-stemming from the fact that we have grafted the 'wrong' corollas to each leaf when forming the position of y ⊳ y on the left. It is important to note, however, that this is nevertheless part of the data of ⊳ , and we do not yet know how to tell Poly to rule it out.

So ⊳ is carrying around extraneous-even misleading-data about how our dynamical system behaves when the state system moves in the right direction but to the wrong state. Our isomorphism -1 is a temporary fi x, but as we pointed out in Example 4.43, it relies on the settheoretic equality of the direction-sets of -there is nothing inherent to the categorical structure of in Poly that encodes how directions map to states, at least not yet.

The key to resolving both these issues lies in the next section, where we study lenses whose codomains are composite polynomials and extend our polybox notation to depict them.

## 6.2 Lenses to composites

Lenses to composites-that is, lenses of the form : → 1 ⊳ · · · ⊳ for some ∈ N with composites as their codomains-will be ubiquitous in the remainder of our story. In particular, they have nice properties that make them convenient to work with, especially using polyboxes.

We could try to write out the dependent functions that these instructions correspond to. Alternatively, we could simply draw this protocol out using polyboxes, with every 'for each' step corresponding to a blue box to be fi lled by the user and every 'choose' step corresponding to a white box to be fi lled by the protocol:

Whenever we draw two pairs of polyboxes on top of each other, as we do with the polyboxes for 1 and 2 above on the right, we are indicating that the entire column of polyboxes depicts the composite of the polynomials depicted by each individual pair. So the column of polyboxes on the right represents the composite 1 ⊳ 2 . In particular, the entry 2 ∈ 2 ( 1 ) in the position box of the top pair is associated with the entry 1 ∈ 1 [ 1 ] in the direction box of the bottom pair, in accordance with the depicted position of the composite.

So a lens → 1 ⊳ 2 is any protocol that will fi ll in the white boxes above as the user fi lls in the blue boxes in the direction of the arrows, starting as always from the bottom left and ending as always at the top left. We will see this in action in Example 6.39.

In fact, (3.34), (3.13), and (6.38) are the respective polybox depictions of the = 0 , = 1 , and = 2 cases of lenses → 1 ⊳ · · · ⊳ to -fold composites (we consider the monoidal unit y of ⊳ to be the 0-fold composite, and a 1-fold composite is just a polynomial on its own). In general, for any ∈ N , we have

so the polybox depiction of → 1 ⊳ · · · ⊳ generalizes analogously. For example, here are the polyboxes for a lens to a 4-fold composite:

These lenses to -fold composites lend themselves to a natural interpretation as interaction protocols. Each -position is passed forward to a 1 -position. For every direction at that 1 -position, there is then also a 2 -position. Then for every direction at that 2 -position, there is a 3 -position, and so on, all the way until a direction has been selected at a -position. Together, all the directions chosen from 1 , . . . , then inform the direction that is selected at the original -position. A lens → 1 ⊳ · · · ⊳ is therefore a multi-step protocol for to make decisions by asking for decisions from 1 , then 2 , etc., all the way to , then interpreting the results.

Example 6.40 (Dynamical systems with composite interfaces) . We explored dynamical systems with product interfaces in Section 4.3.1 and parallel product interfaces in Section 4.3.2. Now have all the tools we need to characterize dynamical systems with composite interfaces.

By the previous example, a dynamical system : y → ⊳

can be drawn as

![[Polynomial Functors_ A Mathematical Theory of Interaction.assets/figure-0168.png]]

We can interpret the behavior of this system as follows. Rather than a single interface ⊳ , we view as having two interfaces that must be interacted with in succession, followed by .

Given the current state ∈ , the system feeds it into to return a position of the fi rst interface . Upon receiving a direction ∈ [ ] , it then uses to return another position (dependent on the state and the direction ), this time belonging to the second interface . Once a second direction is received, this time from [ ] , the system updates its state by feeding the current state and the pair of directions ( , ) it received into ♯ , yielding a new state ∈ .

By following the arrows in the polybox picture, we see that a dynamical system with a composite interface captures a very natural type of interaction. We could use to model a system displaying cascading menus, where selecting option on the fi rst menu opens a second menu . After the interacting agent selects option from the second menu, both choices are sent back to the state system, which updates its state accordingly.

All this generalizes to -fold composite interfaces exactly how you would expect: a dynamical system with interface 1 ⊳ · · · ⊳ returns a position and receives a direction through interface 1 , then accordingly returns a position and receives a direction through interface 2 , and so on, until it returns a position (according to the current state and all previous directions) and receives a direction through , whereupon it updates its state according to the directions it received along with the current state.

## 6.2.2 The composition product of lenses as polyboxes

A special case of a lens whose codomain is a composite is a lens that is itself the composition product of lenses. If we draw such a lens using polyboxes by following the instructions from (6.19) and (6.20), we would just be stacking the polyboxes for the constituent lenses atop each other. For example, given lenses : → and ′ : ′ → ′ , here is ⊳ ′ drawn as polyboxes:

![[Polynomial Functors_ A Mathematical Theory of Interaction.assets/figure-0169.png]]

What distinguishes this from simply writing down the polyboxes for and the polyboxes for ′ is that we are explictly associating the position in the lower box of ′ with the direction in the upper box of ; likewise, we are associating the position in the lower box of ′ with the direction in the upper box of . We should think of the user as fi lling out the lower set of boxes fi rst and working their way up, so that, in particular, they may use the behavior of 1 and ♯ to decide what to put in the position box of ′ . So this really does depict a lens ⊳ ′ → ⊳ ′ .

Let us consider how (6.41) extends our polybox depiction of a lens to a composite from (6.38), but with the domain also replaced with a composite. A user who interacts with (6.41) can fi ll the lower set of polyboxes (the ones for ) fi rst, ignoring the upper set of polyboxes (the ones for ′ ) until the entire lower half is fi lled. Alternatively, after they fi ll the position box of , but before they fi ll anything else, they can already decide what position to put in the lower box of ′ for every possible direction that could fi ll the upper box of . By (6.15), such a choice is equivalent to picking a position of the composite ⊳ ′ . Then by (6.19), following the bottom arrow 1 leads to the corresponding position of given by ⊳ ′ , while fi lling in the direction box of and following ♯ , then ′ 1 leads to the position of ′ that goes in the bottom box of ′ . Finally, once the user fi lls in the direction box of ′ , following the top arrow ( ′ ) ♯ completes the specification of a direction of ⊳ ′ . In this way, (6.41) can be thought of as a special case of (6.38).

Example 6.43 (Transition lenses for state systems) . In Example 6.42, we saw that the 2-fold composition product ⊳ 2 of a dynamical system : y → can be drawn as follows:

![[Polynomial Functors_ A Mathematical Theory of Interaction.assets/figure-0173.png]]

This almost models the behavior of running through the system twice, except that we should only have one blue box on the domain side-the one we fi ll with the initial state 0 . The second blue box on the domain side, the one we fi ll with 1 , should instead be fi lled automatically with the same state as the direction 1 in the white box below it.

In fact, we would like to construct a lens y → ⊳ that takes an initial state 0 ∈ and runs the original system twice, returning two positions and receiving two directions before stopping at the new state 2 . But we just learned how to take a composition product of lenses such as ⊳ 2 : y ⊳ y → ⊳ and convert its domain to a new polynomial, say y , with only one blue box on the domain side-we can compose it with another lens y → y ⊳ y like Note the crucial role 1 plays: it assigns every direction at a given state to the new state that the direction in question should point to, encoding how the state system transitions from one state to the next. We have been labeling each direction by the state that it points to, but we should really think of all the directions of y as pairs of states ( , ) ∈ × , where ( , ) is a direction at state-asposition that represents the transition from state to state . The structure of the polynomial y tells us the state that each direction transitions from, but it is 1 that tells us the new state 1 ( , ) = that ( , ) transitions to. For that reason, we call the transition lens of the state system y , and we call 1 : × → the target function , relabeling it tgt for short, indicating where each direction leads.

![[Polynomial Functors_ A Mathematical Theory of Interaction.assets/figure-0174.png]]

This is exactly what we wanted back in Example 4.43: a way to encode which directions of a state system point to which positions in the language of Poly . Expressions in that language are lenses such as , and polyboxes like the ones above are the way we write them down.

We highlighted 1 above, but the arrows 0 and 2 are no less important. As an identity function, 0 = id remembers the initial state 0 as we shift from its original setting y , where we can only move one state away from 0 , to a new setting y ⊳ y , where we can think about all the ways to move two states away from 0 . Meanwhile, 2 shifts us from the two-state setting back to the onestate setting while ensuring a sort of transitive coherence condition: the state where we end up after moving from 0 through 1 to 2 is the same state we would end up at if we had moved from 0 directly to 2 . We will call it the run function , because it runs a sequence of two transitions together, and because it is what keeps the system running as it tells the original state system to actually move along one of its directions.

We draw the polyboxes picture of the transition lens of y Example 6.43 resolves the second issue we raised in Section 6.1.4, page 250 for the case of = 2, giving us a dynamical system # ( ⊳ ) : y → ⊳ that simulates stepping through the system twice. It remains to consider more general values of .

![[Polynomial Functors_ A Mathematical Theory of Interaction.assets/figure-0175.png]]

The = 0 case is modeled by the do-nothing section : y → y . But there is some overlap in how matches up state-positions with directions and how does. Put another way, if tells us how to do nothing, and tells us how to do two things, then we had better check that if one of the two things we do is nothing, then that is the same as doing just one thing. We must ensure that and agree on what they are saying about our state system.

Then for = 3, we would like a dynamical system y → ⊳ ⊳ that simulates stepping through three times. One way to do this �  would be to compose ⊳ 3 with ⊳ a lens of the form 3 y → y that we obtain by extending from modeling two-step transitions to threestep transitions. But there are two ways to derive a lens with codomain �  ⊳ 3 �  y from : we could either take # ⊳ id , or we could take �  y # id y ⊳ . For larger values of , there are even more possibilities for what we could do, yet there should only be one dynamical system that models stepping through a fi xed number of times. We need to guarantee that all these different ways of extending to -step transitions end up expressing the same process.

In summary, we need a compatibility condition between and as well an associativity condition on to guarantee that it can be extended coherently. In fact, we already have all the tools we need to characterize these conditions: we will see exactly how to state the properties we want in the next chapter.

## 6.3 Categorical properties of the composition product

We conclude this chapter by discussing several properties of the composition product, many of which we will leverage in the following chapters. We will focus on how ⊳ interacts with the other constructions on Poly that we have previously introduced.

## 6.3.1 Interaction with products and coproducts

The composition product behaves well with products and coproducts on the left.

Proposition 6.46 (Left distributivity of ⊳ over + and × ) . Given a polynomial , the functor (-⊳ ) : Poly → Poly that sends each ∈ Poly to ⊳ commutes with coproducts and products (up to natural isomorphism). That is, for any , ∈ Poly , we have the following natural isomorphisms:

![[Polynomial Functors_ A Mathematical Theory of Interaction.assets/formula-0491.png]]

![[Polynomial Functors_ A Mathematical Theory of Interaction.assets/formula-0492.png]]

and

![[Polynomial Functors_ A Mathematical Theory of Interaction.assets/formula-0493.png]]

More generally, given a set and polynomials ( ) ∈ , we have the following natural isomorphisms:

![[Polynomial Functors_ A Mathematical Theory of Interaction.assets/formula-0494.png]]

![[Polynomial Functors_ A Mathematical Theory of Interaction.assets/formula-0495.png]]

Proof. Formally, this follows from the fact that (co)products of functors Set → Set are computed pointwise (Proposition 1.37) and that (co)products in Poly coincide with (co)products in Set Set (Propositions 3.3 and 3.56). One could instead give an explicit proof using (6.5); this is done in Exercise 6.51. In fact, we will see yet another proof of (6.50) (and thus of (6.48)) in Exercise 6.69 #2. □

## 6.3.2 Interaction with limits on the left

We saw in Theorem 5.34 that Poly has all limits, and we saw in Exercise 5.43 that these limits coincide with limits in Set Set . Hence the argument in the proof of Proposition 6.46 by appealing to Proposition 1.37 can be generalized to arbitrary limits. It follows that ⊳ preserves all limits on the left. But we will present a proof of this fact from an alternative perspective: by appealing to the left coclosure of ⊳ .

Example 6.66 (Dynamical systems as coalgebras) . Taking B B ∈ Set in (6.64), we find that there is a natural isomorphism between dynamical systems y → and functions → ( ) . Such a function is known as a coalgebra for the functor or a -5 coalgebra .

Coalgebras as models of dynamical systems have been studied extensively in the context of computer science, most notably by Jacobs in [Jac17]. Indeed, much of what we developed in Sections 4.1 and 4.2 stems from the theory of coalgebras. The coalgebraic perspective has the benefit of staying in the familiar category of sets; moreover, it can be generalized to functors Set → Set that are not polynomial, although many of the interesting examples are.

On the other hand, we have already seen that viewing dynamical systems as lenses y → rather than as functions → ( ) has the benefit of isolating the internal state system to the domain and the external interface to the codomain, aiding both intuition and functionality. Additionally, our adjunction lets us switch to the coalgebraic perspective whenever we see fi t: Poly lets us talk about both.

Proposition 6.67 (Left preservation of limits) . The operation ⊳ preserves limits on the left (up to natural isomorphism). That is, if J is a category, -: J → Poly is a functor, and ∈ Poly is a polynomial, then there is a natural isomorphism

5 There are two versions of coalgebras we are interested in (and more that we are not) with distinct definitions: a coalgebra for a functor , which is the version used here, and a coalgebra for a comonad , which is a coalgebra for a functor with extra conditions that we will introduce later in Section 7.3.3. The version we are using will usually be clear from context-here, for example, we do not expect to be a comonad-but we will try to be explicit with our terminology whenever the interpretation may be ambiguous.

![[Polynomial Functors_ A Mathematical Theory of Interaction.assets/formula-0500.png]]

Proof. By Proposition 6.56, the functor (-⊳ ) : Poly → Poly is the right - adjoint of the functor : Poly → -Poly , and right adjoints preserve limits. □

## 6.3.3 Interaction with limits on the right

We have seen that ⊳ preserves limits on the left; on the other hand, Exercise 6.55 shows that ⊳ does not even preserve products on the right, so it certainly does not preserve all limits. But it turns out that there is a special class of limits that ⊳ does preserve on the right.

Definition 6.70 (Connected limit) . A connected limit is one whose indexing category J is nonempty and connected. That is, J has at least one object, and any two objects are connected by a fi nite zigzag of arrows.

Example 6.71 . The following categories are connected:

![[Polynomial Functors_ A Mathematical Theory of Interaction.assets/figure-0185.png]]

![[Polynomial Functors_ A Mathematical Theory of Interaction.assets/figure-0186.png]]

![[Polynomial Functors_ A Mathematical Theory of Interaction.assets/figure-0187.png]]

![[Polynomial Functors_ A Mathematical Theory of Interaction.assets/figure-0188.png]]

In particular, equalizers, pullbacks, and directed limits are examples of connected limits.

The following categories are not connected:

![[Polynomial Functors_ A Mathematical Theory of Interaction.assets/figure-0189.png]]

In particular, terminal objects and products are not examples of connected limits.

Connected limits are intimately related to slice categories, which we defined back in Definition 5.79. For example, products in a slice category C / are just pullbacks in C , allowing us to view a non-connected limit as a connected one. By relating Poly to its slice categories via an adjunction, we can show that ⊳ preserves connected limits. (An alternative proof of this fact can be found in [GK12, Proposition 1.16].)

Recall that objects in a slice category C / are just morphisms with codomain . For ease of notation, we will often suppress the actual morphism and just write down the name of its domain when there is a canonical choice for the morphism, or when it is clear from context. So for example, on the left-hand side of (6.73) below, represents the lens : → ⊳ 1 and ⊳ represents the lens ⊳ ! : ⊳ → ⊳ 1 , both objects in the slice category Poly / ⊳ 1 .

Proposition 6.72. Given polynomials , , ∈ Poly and a lens : → ⊳ 1 , there is a natural isomorphism

Theorem 6.79 (Preservation of connected limits) . The operation ⊳ preserves connected limits on both sides. That is, if J is a connected category, : J → Poly is a functor, and ∈ Poly is a polynomial, then there are natural isomorphisms

![[Polynomial Functors_ A Mathematical Theory of Interaction.assets/formula-0504.png]]

Proof. The claim for the left side is just a special case of Proposition 6.67; it remains to prove the claim on the right.

By Theorem 5.34, Poly is complete, so by [nLa19, Theorem 4.3], 6 it suffices to show that the functor ( ⊳ -) : Poly → Poly preserves wide pullbacks on the right. By Proposition 6.72, the functor ( ⊳ -) : Poly → Poly / ⊳ 1 is a right adjoint, so it preserves limits, including wide pullbacks. Thus ( ⊳ -) sends a wide pullback over ∈ Poly to a wide pullback over the canonical lens ⊳ → ⊳ 1 in Poly / ⊳ 1 , corresponding to a limit in Poly of a diagram consisting of arrows to ⊳ and arrows to ⊳ 1 factoring through ⊳ . So up to isomorphism, this limit is a wide pullback in Poly over ⊳ , namely ( ⊳ -) : Poly → Poly applied to the original wide pullback. So ⊳ preserves wide pullbacks on the right. □

Example 6.84 (Lenses from ⊗ to ⊳ ) . For any and , there is an interesting cartesian lens , : ⊗ → ⊳ that, stated informally, 'orders' the operation, taking the symmetric monoidal product ⊗ and reinterprets it as a special case of the asymmetric monoidal product ⊳ . Defining this lens in the usual way is tedious and unilluminating, but written in polyboxes, the lens looks like this (recall that positions of ⊗ are just pairs of positions of and , while directions at each such pair are pairs of directions of and , one at each position in the pair; we drop the parentheses around the ordered pair for readability):

![[Polynomial Functors_ A Mathematical Theory of Interaction.assets/figure-0193.png]]

Usually, the positions box of is allowed to depend on the directions box of in the polyboxes for ⊳ on its own. But in the polyboxes above, is not allowed to depend on in ⊗ on the left, so the arrow from the positions box of to the directions box of on the right doesn't actually take into account at all. So the lens , is in some sense the inclusion of the order-independent positions of ⊳ ; when drawn as trees, the positions in its image are the ones whose upper-level corollas are all the same. And of course we can flip the order using the symmetry ⊗  ⊗ . This is, we just as well have a lens ⊗ → ⊳ .

Both ⊗ and ⊳ have the same monoidal unit, the identity functor y , whose identity is the unique lens y → y . In fact the lenses , constitute a lax monoidal functor ( Poly , y , ⊗) → ( Poly , y , ⊳ ) . In particular, , commutes with associators and unitors.

This can be used in the following way. Lenses → ⊳ into composites are fairly easy to understand (through polyboxes, for example), whereas lenses ⊳ → are not so easy to think about. However, given such a lens, one may always compose it with , to obtain a lens ⊗ → . This is quite a bit simpler to think about: they are our familiar interaction patterns from Section 4.4.

It turns out that the monoidal structures ⊗ and ⊳ together satisfy an interesting property known as duoidality . We will not give the full definition of what it means for two monoidal structures to be duoidal here-there are a few commutative diagrams to verify for technical reasons-but the key condition is that there is a natural lens

![[Polynomial Functors_ A Mathematical Theory of Interaction.assets/formula-0507.png]]

Proposition6.86. Themonoidalstructures ( y , ⊗) and ( y , ⊳ ) together comprise a duoidal structure on Poly .

## 6.3.5 Interaction with vertical and cartesian lenses

We conclude this section by examining how ⊳ interacts with vertical and cartesian lenses, as defined in Definition 5.52.

Proposition 6.87 (Preservation of cartesian lenses) . If : → ′ and : ′ → are cartesian lenses, then so is ⊳ : ⊳ → ′ ⊳ ′ .

Proof. We use the third characterization of cartesian lenses given in Proposition 5.65, as lenses whose naturality squares are pullbacks. For any sets , and function ℎ : → , consider the diagram

![[Polynomial Functors_ A Mathematical Theory of Interaction.assets/figure-0196.png]]

The square on the left is a pullback because : → ′ is cartesian. Meanwhile, the square on the right is a pullback because : → ′ is cartesian and ⊳ preserves pullbacks by Theorem 6.79. Hence the outer rectangle is a pullback as well, implying that ⊳ : ⊳ → ′ ⊳ ′ is cartesian. □

Exercise 6.88 . Let : → ′ and : → ′ be lenses.

2. Find a vertical lens and a polynomial for which the lens ⊳ : ⊳ → ′ ⊳ is not vertical.

## 6.4 Summary and further reading

In this chapter we introduced the composition (sometimes called 'substitution') product ⊳ . Given polynomials , thought of as functors, their composite is again polynomial and is given by ⊳ . We explained how it looks in terms of algebra, e.g. how to compute y 2 ⊳ ( y + 1 ) ; in ÍÎÍÎ terms of sets, e.g. as a sum-product-sum-product , which can ÍÎ be reduced to a single ; in terms of trees, by stacking corollas on top of corollas; and in terms of polyboxes. We paid particular attention to lenses into a composite → ⊳ .

This allowed us to explain how to think of dynamical systems y → ⊳ with composite interfaces as multi-step machines: each state produces a -position , and then for every [ ] -direction produces a -position , and fi nally for every [ ] -direction returns an updated state in .

Finally, we discussed some facts of the composition product. For - example, we showed that -⊳ has a left adjoint and that ⊳ --has a left multi-adjoint -⌢ . We also explained that ⊳ preserves all with limits in the variable and all connected limits in the variable . We also explained the duoidal interaction between ⊗ and ⊳ , i.e. the natural lens ⊳ ⊗ ⊳ →⊗ ⊳ ⊗ , and how ⊳ interacts with cartesian lenses.

Polynomial composition is one of the best known aspects of polynomial functors. Again, see [GK12] for more on this. We learned of the left coclosure (see Proposition 6.56) from Josh Meyers, though it may have already been known in the containers community.

## Solution to Exercise 6.45.

We give two reasonable (and equivalent) ways to interpret the transition lens : y → y ⊳ y .

�  One wa �  y is to fi rst evaluate its interface as ⊳   × y y y y × . Then we see that if the current state is 0 ∈ , the system returns a position consisting of that current state 0 along with a function → , namely the identity function 1 ↦→ 1 . The system then takes as its direction a pair ( 1 , 2 ) ∈ × , discarding 1 and setting its new state to be 2 .

Alternatively, we can draw from Example 6.40 to interpret as a dynamical system that behaves as follows. Each run through the system is a 2-step process: first, the current state 0 ∈ is returned, and a new state 1 ∈ is received. Then this new state 1 is immediately returned, and an even newer state 2 ∈ is received. Then the current state is updated to the newer state 2 .

## 7. Polynomial comonoids and retrofunctors

One of the most surprising aspects of Poly is its comonoid objects. In 2018, Daniel Ahman and Tarmo Uustalu characterized comonoids in ( Poly , y , ⊳ ) as something surprisingly familiar: they are in fact small categories. For us, this story will emerge naturally as we expand our understanding of the state system of a dependent dynamical system.

## 7.1 State systems, categorically

Since defining dependent dynamical systems in Definition 4.18, we have evolved our understanding of their state systems over the course of the last few chapters. Let us take this moment to review what we know about these state systems so far.

Our original definition of a state system was as a monomial y for some set . But in Example 4.43, we noted that this formulation requires us to discuss the positions and directions of a state system at the level of sets rather than in the language of Poly . Instead, let's take an arbitrary polynomial ∈ Poly and attempt to characterize what it means for to be a state system using only the categorical machinery of Poly . We will continue to refer to the positions of as states , but we will shift from thinking of the directions of as states to thinking of them as transitions from one state to another.

## 7.1.1 The do-nothing section

In Example 4.43, we saw that every state system is equipped with a do-nothing section : a lens : → y that picks out a direction at each state that we would like to interpret as 'doing nothing' and remaining at that state.

We drew in polyboxes in Example 4.45, but that was when we let ourselves assume that the position-set of a state system was equal to each of its direction-sets. Now all we know is that for each state ∈ ( 1 ) , the do-nothing section chooses an [ ] -direction to signify staying at the same state; it does not make sense to say that this direction is literally equal to . So we need a different name for the [ ] -direction that identifies: call it id , because it behaves like a sort of identity operation on the state .

So the revised polyboxes for the do-nothing section : → y are as follows:

![[Polynomial Functors_ A Mathematical Theory of Interaction.assets/formula-0546.png]]

Exercise 7.2 . Say I have a polynomial ∈ Poly , and I tell you that there is a lens : → y . What can you say about the polynomial ?

Example 7.3 (The do-nothing section in tree pictures) . We have seen the do-nothing section drawn in polyboxes, but let us see what it looks like in our tree pictures. We take B { ■ , ▲ , } y { ■ , ▲ , } , drawn as follows, with the directions associated with ■ , ▲ , and from left to right:

![[Polynomial Functors_ A Mathematical Theory of Interaction.assets/figure-0216.png]]

Then the do-nothing section : → y can be drawn as follows:

![[Polynomial Functors_ A Mathematical Theory of Interaction.assets/figure-0217.png]]

It picks out one direction at each position, namely the one with the same color.

There is not much else we can say about the do-nothing section on its own, so let us revisit the other lens that every state system is equipped with before considering the relationship between the two.

## 7.1.2 The transition lens

We saw in Example 6.43 that also comes equipped with a transition lens : a lens : → ⊳ , which we can draw as

![[Polynomial Functors_ A Mathematical Theory of Interaction.assets/figure-0218.png]]

The arrow labeled tgt is the target function : given a state 0 and a direction 1 at that state, tgt ( 0 , 1 ) tells us the new state 1 that following 1 from 0 will lead to. We know that when 0 is fi xed, the target function on the second component 1 should be an isomorphism [ 0 ]→ ( 1 ) ; that is, there is exactly one direction at 0 that leads to each state of . But this property is a little tricky to state in the language of Poly ; in fact, we will not attempt to do so just yet. Instead, we will use it to make a notational choice: given , ∈ , we will let → denote the unique direction at that leads to , so that tgt ( , → ) = . So we can redraw our transition lens as

![[Polynomial Functors_ A Mathematical Theory of Interaction.assets/figure-0219.png]]

In addition to the fact that tgt ( 0 , 0 → 1 ) = 1 as intended, this picture tells us two more properties of .

The fi rst is that the bottom arrow is the identity on ( 1 ) . This is something we would like to be able to express categorically in the language of Poly . We will see that this property falls out naturally when we express how the transition lens plays nicely with the do-nothing section in Section 7.1.3.

The second is that the run arrow, which runs the transition 0 → 1 and the transition 1 → 2 together into a transition starting at 0 , should have the same target as the second transition it follows: in this case, 2 . Equationally, writing the left and right-hand sides only in terms of the contents of the blue boxes, we have that

![[Polynomial Functors_ A Mathematical Theory of Interaction.assets/formula-0547.png]]

We will see that this property arises naturally when we we generalize the transition lens to more than two steps in Section 7.1.4.

Example 7.6 (The transition lens in tree pictures) . Continuing from Example 7.3, we draw the transition lens 3 : ⊳ of B 3 → y  { ■ , ▲ , } y { ■ , ▲ , } (where directions are labeled with their targets) in tree pictures as well, recalling that the trees of ⊳ are obtained by taking an -corolla and grafting more -corollas to each of its leaves:

![[Polynomial Functors_ A Mathematical Theory of Interaction.assets/figure-0220.png]]

On positions, the target function of tells us which root of to graft onto each leaf of . Then on directions, the run function of tells us how to collapse the height-2 leaves of the trees we obtain in ⊳ down to the original height-1 leaves of the corollas of .

We can draw what the target function is doing more compactly by taking the corollas of and 'bending the arrows' so that they point to their targets, like so:

![[Polynomial Functors_ A Mathematical Theory of Interaction.assets/figure-0221.png]]

So the target function of turns our corolla picture of into a complete graph on its roots. Then the run function takes any two arrows that form a path in the graph and collapses them down to a single arrow that starts (and, according to (7.5), ends) at the same vertex as the two-arrow path.

## 7.1.3 The do-nothing section coheres with the transition lens

For each state ∈ ( 1 ) , the do-nothing section : → y picks out the [ ] -direction id that 'does nothing' and keeps the system in the same state . But it is the transition lens : → ⊳ that actually sets our state system in motion, specifying the target of each direction and how two directions run together. Either of these directions could be our donothing direction id , so let us determine what should happen when we set each one in turn to id .

We can draw in polyboxes what happens when we set id , as specified by , to be the fi rst direction that runs together like this:

![[Polynomial Functors_ A Mathematical Theory of Interaction.assets/figure-0222.png]]

Reading this picture from left to right, we see that it depicts the polyboxes of the composite lens # ( ⊳ ) : → y ⊳  (recall that we sometimes denote the identity lens on also by ). To make this interpretation more transparent, we could be a little more verbose with our polybox picture if we wanted to (omitting the contents of the boxes for clarity):

![[Polynomial Functors_ A Mathematical Theory of Interaction.assets/figure-0223.png]]

Now what should tgt ( id ) be, and what should go in the direction box on the left?

If following the direction id from the state is the same as doing nothing, then its target state should be the same state that it emerged from. Moreover, running together id with any other direction → from should be no different from the direction → on its own. So

![[Polynomial Functors_ A Mathematical Theory of Interaction.assets/formula-0548.png]]

In fact, id should just be the direction → . Pictorially, we have the equation Or, if you prefer, we might say that # ( ⊳ ) = id , or that the following diagram commutes:

⊳

This commutative diagram captures one way in which and always relate-and it is written entirely in the language of Poly , without having to talk about individual sets!

What about setting the second direction that runs together to what is specified by , rather than the fi rst? To answer this, we should look at the composite lens # ( ⊳ ) : → ⊳ y  instead. But the do-nothing direction should still do nothing, so here is what the polybox picture should look like:

![[Polynomial Functors_ A Mathematical Theory of Interaction.assets/figure-0226.png]]

The lens depicted on the right-hand side of the equation is again the identity lens on .

If we match up the two white boxes on the right-hand side of the equation with the corresponding white boxes on the left, we can actually read two equations from this polybox picture. Matching up positions in the codomain tells us that the bottom arrow of on the left must send to itself: it is the identity function on ( 1 ) . Indeed, this is exactly what we wanted to say about that arrow in Section 7.1.2.

Meanwhile, matching up directions in the domain tells us that

as we would expect: id is just be the direction → .

More concisely, we can express both these facts in Poly via the equation # ( ⊳ ) = id . The corresponding commutative diagram is as follows:

## 7.1.4 The transition lens is coassociative

Shortly after Example 6.43, we noted that while the transition lens : → ⊳ gives us a canonical way to model two steps of a dynamical system with state system , we have a choice of how to model three steps through the same system: we could obtain a lens → ⊳ 3 that runs three directions together by taking either one of the composite lenses # ( ⊳ ) or # ( ⊳ ) . That presents a problem for us: which one should we choose?

Happily, it turns out this choice is a false one. If we write out the two composite lenses in polyboxes, with # ( ⊳ ) on the left and # ( ⊳ ) on the right, we fi nd that they are equal:

![[Polynomial Functors_ A Mathematical Theory of Interaction.assets/figure-0229.png]]

(7.8)

Remember: the way to read these polyboxes is to start at the lower blue square on the left and follow the path counter clockwise around the diagram; and if you reach a box with no arrows leading out of it, go up to the blue box above it and continue to follow the arrows from there.

There is a lot going on here, so let us break it down-we will focus on the run functions fi rst. On the left-hand side, we run together 0 → 1 and 1 → 2 to obtain 0 → 2 , before running that together with 2 → 3 to obtain 0 → 3 , as we see in the upper left box. Meanwhile, on the right, we run together 1 → 2 and 2 → 3 to obtain 1 → 3 , before running 0 → 1 together with our newly obtained 1 → 3 to again obtain 0 → 3 in the upper left box. We could write this all out equationally, but all this is saying is that 'running together' the directions of a state system is an associative operation. When running together three directions, it does not matter whether we run the fi rst two together or the last two together to start. Not only is this guaranteed by the way in which we constructed , it also makes intuitive sense.

Exercise 7.9 . Using only the contents of the blue boxes and the target and run functions, write down the equation that we can read from (7.8) expressing the associativity of the 'running together' operation.

This associative property is what we get by matching up the white direction boxes on each domain side, but there are three more white position boxes on each codomain side that we can match up as well. The fact that the lower two of these pairs coincide is a consequence of the fact that the bottom arrow of is the identity, which we already knew from Section 7.1.3; so we do not learn anything new there. On the other hand, the fact that both the upper position boxes in the codomain contain 2 implies that

![[Polynomial Functors_ A Mathematical Theory of Interaction.assets/formula-0550.png]]

![[Polynomial Functors_ A Mathematical Theory of Interaction.assets/formula-0551.png]]

which is exactly what we wanted in (7.5). In words, this says that when we run together 0 → 1 and 1 → 2 , the new direction's target is the same as the direction of 1 → 2 , the latter of the two directions that we ran together. Again, this coincides with our intuition: if we follow two directions in order, we should end up at wherever the latter direction leads us.

Hence both the associativity of running directions together and the relationship between the target and run functions from (7.5) are captured by the equality of lenses # ( ⊳ ) = # ( ⊳ ) . Equivalently, the following diagram in Poly commutes:

![[Polynomial Functors_ A Mathematical Theory of Interaction.assets/figure-0230.png]]

Another way to say this is that is coassociative : while is only a lens → ⊳ 2 as defined, the commutativity of (7.10) tells us that the two ways of getting a lens → ⊳ 3 out of are actually the same. (This is dual to an associative operation, which is a binary operation that gives rise to two identical ternary operations.)

So induces a canonical lens → ⊳ 3 , which we will call ( 3 ) , as it has 3 copies of in its codomain. Armed with this new lens, we can model three steps through a system : → with interface ∈ Poly as the composite lens

![[Polynomial Functors_ A Mathematical Theory of Interaction.assets/formula-0552.png]]

In fact, coassociativity guarantees that induces a canonical lens ( ) : → ⊳ for every integer ≥ 2, starting with ( 2 ) B . 1 For concreteness, we could then define ( ) for &gt; 2 inductively by ( ) B # ( ( -1 ) ⊳ ) , or just as well by ( ) B # ( ⊳ ( -1 ) ) or even ( ) B # ( ( ℓ ) ⊳ ( ) ) for some pair of integers ℓ , &gt; 1 satisfying ℓ + = .

1 Perhaps this notation seems a little unnatural, but it helps to think of the original : → ⊳ as the = 2 case of a generalized transition lens modeling steps through the state system.

Regardless, the coassociativity of means that it doesn't matter how we build a lens → ⊳ ( + 1 ) out of , # , ⊳ , and identity lenses: we will always end up with the same lens. We will state this in more generality in Proposition 7.20, but here is some practice with the = 4 case for a taste of what's to come.

## 7.1.5 Running dynamical systems

Finally, we are ready to fulfill our promise from way back in Example 4.43 by using the language of Poly to describe stepping through a dynamical system times for arbitrary ∈ N . Given a dynamical system : → with interface ∈ Poly , we can construct a new dynamical system that we call Run ( ) , with the same state system but a new interface ⊳ , by defining Run ( ) B ( ) # ⊳ . Visually, we define Run ( ) so that the following diagram commutes:

![[Polynomial Functors_ A Mathematical Theory of Interaction.assets/figure-0231.png]]

One way to think of this is that Run ( ) is a sped-up version of : one step through Run ( ) is equivalent to steps through . But this is just because a single interaction with the interface ⊳ models a sequence of interactions with the interface , as detailed in Section 6.1.4 and Example 6.42. So Run ( ) repackages cycles through into a single step. Crucially, ( ) is what tells us how to sequence all of these steps together on the state system side. We illustrated how does this for the = 2 case in Example 6.43, and here is a polybox picture for the = 3

![[Polynomial Functors_ A Mathematical Theory of Interaction.assets/figure-0232.png]]

decimal point; we'd like to do away with all these 0's. In other words, we want a new system y → N y that acts like , except that it only returns every other position that returns.

We could build such a system from scratch-or we can simply start from and apply Run2, yielding a system Run2 ( ) : y → N y ⊳ N y  2 N y that returns the positions of two at a time:

![[Polynomial Functors_ A Mathematical Theory of Interaction.assets/formula-0553.png]]

Then we just need to compose Run2 ( ) with a lens 2 2 : N y → N y equal to the second coordinate projection on positions (and the identity on directions) to extract the positions we want. The new system y → N y that skips over every position of is therefore the following composite:

![[Polynomial Functors_ A Mathematical Theory of Interaction.assets/figure-0233.png]]

We can apply this technique in general to skip (or otherwise act on) the positions of a dynamical system at regular intervals.

One drawback of the Run (-) operation is that we need to keep track of a separate morphism y → ⊳ for every ∈ N , as well as various ways to relate these morphisms for different values of . Is there a way to package all this information into a single morphism that can model arbitrarily long runs through the system? We will answer this question in Chapter 8; but for now, let us investigate what is really going on with our state systems algebraically.

## 7.1.6 State systems as comonoids

It turns out that objects equipped with morphisms like those in Sections 7.1.1 and 7.1.2 that satisfy the commutative diagrams from Sections 7.1.3 and 7.1.4 are well-known to category theorists.

Definition 7.14 (Comonoid) . In a monoidal category ( C , y , ⊳ ) , a comonoid C B ( , , ) consists of

- an object ∈ C , called the carrier ;
- a morphism : → y in C , called the eraser (or the counit ); and
- a morphism : → ⊳ in C , called the duplicator (or the comultiplication );

such that the following diagrams, collectively known as the comonoid laws , commute:

![[Polynomial Functors_ A Mathematical Theory of Interaction.assets/figure-0234.png]]

where the left triangle is known as the left erasure (or counit ) law and the right triangle is known as the right erasure (or counit ) law ; and

![[Polynomial Functors_ A Mathematical Theory of Interaction.assets/figure-0235.png]]

known as the coassociative law .

We may also say that the eraser and duplicator morphisms comprise a comonoid structure on the carrier, or we may identify a comonoid with its carrier if the eraser and duplicator can be inferred from context.

We refer to a comonoid C in ( Poly , y , ⊳ ) as a polynomial comonoid .

Remark 7.17 . The concept of a comonoid in a monoidal category is dual to that of a monoid , which may be more familiar. Monoids come with unit and multiplication morphisms that point the other way, so named because they generalize the unit and multiplication operations of a monoid in Set . (We will discuss monoids in Set further in Example 7.40.) Prepending 'co-' to each term yields the corresponding terms for comonoids.

⊳

The alternative names eraser for the counit and duplicator for the comultiplication are less standard, but we will favor them to avoid confusion between the counit of a comonoid and the counit of an adjunction -and so that their names match up with the Greek letters and that we will so often use to label them. The word 'duplicator' comes from the fact that : → ⊳ effectively turns one into two, while the 'eraser' : → y erases the altogether, leaving only the monoidal unit y . Still, it can be helpful to think of comonoids as having a coassociative comultiplication along with a counit satisfying left and right counit laws .

Remark 7.18 . Comonoids in a functor category with respect to the composition product are generally known as comonads . So it would be a little more precise and familiar to refer to our polynomial comonoids as polynomial comonads . But since we think of our polynomials more often in terms of positions and directions than as functors, we will favor the term comonoid over comonad.

Example 7.19 (State systems are polynomial comonoids) . Nearly all our work on state systems up until now can be summarized thusly:

every state system is a polynomial comonoid, whose eraser is the do-nothing section and whose duplicator is the transition lens.

The comonoid structure on a state system is what allows us to write canonical lenses → ⊳ for any ∈ N . We can then model steps through a dynamical system : → with interface ∈ Poly by composing this canonical lens with ⊳ to obtain a 'sped-up' dynamical system Run ( ) . This new system has the same state system , but its interface is now ⊳ .

The canonicity of → ⊳ is due to the following standard result about comonoids, which can be proved inductively.

Proposition 7.20 (Defining ( ) ) . Given a comonoid ( , , ) , let ( ) : → ⊳ be given as follows. Let ( 0   ) B and inductively define ( + 1 ) B # ( ) ⊳ for all ∈ N . Then we have the follow-

- (a) ( ) is a morphism → ⊳ for all ∈ N ;
- (d) ( ) = # ( ) ⊳ ( -) for all , ∈ N with ≤ , so our choice of morphism → ⊳ ( + 1 ) is canonical.
- ing: (b) ( 1 ) = = id ; (c) ( 2 ) = ; and  

Proof. We leave parts (a), (b), and (c) for Exercise 7.21. Part (d) amounts to coassociativity. □

We'll continue to use the notation introduced here throughout for general comonoids.
