1. Prove part (a).
2. Prove part (b).
3. Prove part (c).

Example 7.22 (Not all polynomial comonoids are state systems) . At this point, a natural question to ask is whether everything we know about a state system is captured by the fact that state systems are polynomial comonoids. In other words, are state systems the only polynomial comonoids there are?

The answer turns out to be no. After all, there is one fact about state systems from Section 7.1.2 that we did not encode in Poly : for a fi xed state ∈ ( 1 ) , the target function [ ] → ( 1 ) sending directions at to their target states is a bijection.

Nothing in our comonoid laws guarantees this bijectivity. An arbitrary polynomial comonoid might send different directions at to the same target-given a second state , there may be multiple ways to get from to . It might even send no directions at to a target , making it impossible to get from to . (We'll give an explicit example of a comonoid that is not a state system in Example 7.23.) State systems as we have defined them are just the polynomial comonoids that do not allow either of these variations, for which the bijective property holds.

We consider this a feature, not a bug. After all, it is an abstraction to say that there is exactly one way to get from any one state in a system to another. It is perfectly plausible that the inner workings of a state system do not permit traveling between some states and differentiate ways of traveling between others. We won't formally introduce this idea into our theory of dependent dynamical systems, but we will often think of polynomial comonoids as a sort of generalized state system throughout the rest of the book.

Example 7.23 (A comonoid that is not a state system) . The polynomial 2 y + y is not a state system: one of its direction-sets has one fewer element than its position-set. But it can still be given a comonoid structure. We describe that structure here, but we will go a little quickly, because we'll soon discover a much more familiar way to think about comonoids.

Define B { id { } , } id { } y + { } y  2 y + y . Here is its tree picture:

![[Polynomial Functors_ A Mathematical Theory of Interaction.assets/figure-0236.png]]

Notice that we have drawn one direction out of each position-id and id -with a double bar. We let these be the directions that the eraser : → y picks out. The double bar is meant to evoke an equals sign from the root position to the eventual target position, which is appropriate, as these two positions should be equal for every direction that the eraser selects. We can draw the selections that makes like so:

![[Polynomial Functors_ A Mathematical Theory of Interaction.assets/figure-0237.png]]

![[Polynomial Functors_ A Mathematical Theory of Interaction.assets/figure-0238.png]]

Now we need a duplicator : → ⊳ . Before we define it, let us draw out ⊳ to see what it looks like. Remember that we need to graft corollas of onto leaves of in every possible way:

![[Polynomial Functors_ A Mathematical Theory of Interaction.assets/figure-0239.png]]

Each of these trees gives a way to match directions out of one position to positions they could lead to. On positions, will decide which matchings to pick by sending the red to one of the four positions on the left and the blue to one of the two positions on the right. We want the double-barred directions that the eraser picked out to have the same position on either end (in fact, the erasure laws guarantee this). So the only choice to be made is whether we want the other direction at to point to or to . Let us pick for the time being, so that on positions, looks like this:

![[Polynomial Functors_ A Mathematical Theory of Interaction.assets/figure-0240.png]]

As in Example 7.6, we can interpret this as telling us how to 'bend' the arrows of so that they point to other positions:

![[Polynomial Functors_ A Mathematical Theory of Interaction.assets/figure-0241.png]]

Meanwhile, on directions, should tell us how to run two directions together into one. Fortunately, there is not much for us to do here-we know that if one of the two directions runs together is one of the double-barred directions that the eraser picked out, then should ignore that 'do-nothing' direction and yield the other direction (again, the erasure laws ensure this). Here is what that Once you know that state systems are comonoids in Poly , but not the only ones, the natural question to ask is 'what are all the other comonoids in Poly ?' Or perhaps, as we led you through this case study of , you have already suspected the truth: a polynomial comonoidwhat with its directions leading from one position to another, directions that can be run together associatively among which there are directions at every position that do nothing-is just another name for a category.

## 7.2 Polynomial comonoids are categories

What Ahman and Uustalu showed was that polynomial comonoids can be identified with categories. Every category in the usual sense is a comonoid in Poly , and every comonoid in Poly is a category. We fi nd their revelation to be truly shocking, and it suggests some very different ways to think about categories. But let's go over their result fi rst.

Theorem 7.28 (Ahman-Uustalu) . There is a one-to-one isomorphismpreserving correspondence between polynomial comonoids and (small) categories.

Our goal is to spell out this correspondence so that we can justly proclaim:

Comonoids in Poly are precisely categories!

## 7.2.1 Translating between polynomial comonoids and categories

First, we describe how to translate between the carrier of a comonoid C B ( , , ) and the objects and morphisms of the corresponding category C . The idea is pretty simple, and you may have already guessed it: positions are objects and directions are morphisms.

## Positions as objects, directions as morphisms

More precisely, the positions of are the objects of C :

![[Polynomial Functors_ A Mathematical Theory of Interaction.assets/formula-0554.png]]

Then for each such position or object , the [ ] -directions are the morphisms of C with domain :

![[Polynomial Functors_ A Mathematical Theory of Interaction.assets/formula-0555.png]]

The right-hand side above is a little clumsier than the left; this is because while we are used to thinking of hom-sets of categories such as C ( , ) , consisting of all morphisms in C with a fi xed domain and codomain, we aren't used to thinking about the collection of all morphisms in C with a fi xed domain and an arbitrary codomain quite as often. 2 On the other hand, the carrier only encodes which morphisms have each object as its domain, i.e. which directions are at each position. Codomains will be encoded in the data of the comonoid elsewhere.

This is the key difference in perspective between the polynomial comonoid perspective of categories, in contrast to our usual hom-set perspective: the polynomial perspective is in a sense domain-centric, as highlighted by the following definition.

Definition 7.31 (Polynomial carrier) . Let C be a category. For every object in C , denote the morphisms in C with domain by C [ ] , so that 3

![[Polynomial Functors_ A Mathematical Theory of Interaction.assets/formula-0556.png]]

Then the polynomial carrier , or simply carrier , of C is the polynomial

![[Polynomial Functors_ A Mathematical Theory of Interaction.assets/figure-0243.png]]

So everything we have said so far about the correspondence from Theorem 7.28 can be summarized by saying that it preserves carriers: the carrier of the category C is the carrier of the comonoid C , so that Ob C = ( 1 ) and C [ ] = [ ] .

Remark 7.32 . If we take the perspective that categories are equal if and only if their objects and morphisms are equal and obey the same laws, and similarly that polynomials are equal if and only if their positionsets and direction-sets are equal sets, then (7.29) and (7.30) really can be just strict equalities. This is why we are comfortable naming a 'one-toone correspondence' in Theorem 7.28 rather than just, say, some form of equivalence. Since the positions and directions of our polynomials always form sets , however, the categories we obtain under this correspondence are also necessarily small : their objects form a set, as do all of their morphisms. But we won't worry too much about size issues beyond this.

2 Except, perhaps, in the context of coslice categories.

3 We may also write : → \_ to denote an arbitrary morphism ∈ C [ ] , i.e. a morphism in C with domain and an unspecified codomain.

But a category C is more than its carrier polynomial, just as a comonoid C is more than its carrier . In particular, we have said nothing about the codomains of morphisms in C , nor anything about identity morphisms, composition, or how the laws of a category are satisfied. Similarly, we have said nothing about the eraser or the duplicator of C , nor anything about how the comonoid laws are satisfied. It turns out that all of these constituents and laws correspond to one another, as summarized by the following table. Here each item in the comonoid column-either a polynomial, a lens, or a lens equationspans two rows, with the top row corresponding to positions and the bottom row corresponding to directions.

| Comonoid      | C = ( , , )                                 | Category                              | C                                     |
|---------------|---------------------------------------------|---------------------------------------|---------------------------------------|
| carrier       | ∈ ( 1 ) ∈ c [ ]                             | objects morphisms                     | ∈ Ob C : → _                          |
| eraser        | 1 : ( 1 )→ 1 ♯ : 1 → [ ]                    | - identities                          | - id : → _                            |
| duplicator    | 1 : ( 1 )→( ⊳ )( 1 ) ♯ : ( ⊳ )[ 1 ( )]→ [ ] | codomains* composition*               | cod: C [ ]→ Ob C #                    |
| right erasure | right erasure                               | ∗ right identity law                  | ∗ right identity law                  |
| left erasure  | left erasure                                | codomains of identities codid =       | codomains of identities codid =       |
|               |                                             | codomains of composites cod ( ) = cod | codomains of composites cod ( ) = cod |
| coassociative | coassociative                               | #                                     | #                                     |

Note that the on-positions function of , being a function into the terminal set, encodes no actual data. The asterisk ∗ indicates that the right erasure law on positions works together with the duplicator to ensure that codomains and composites are properly specified.

We have already covered the correspondence between the fi rst two rows, so let us consider each of the following rows in turn. In some sense, we have already seen each piece of this correspondence in action for state systems in Section 7.1, so we'll go through it a little faster this time for the general case.

## The eraser assigns identities

We know that the eraser : → y can be identified with a dependent function ( ∈ ( 1 )) → [ ] , sending each position ∈ ( 1 ) to a [ ] -direction. In terms of our category C , the eraser sends each object ∈ Ob C to a morphism → \_. But this is exactly what we need to specify identity morphisms-a morphism out of each object. So the eraser of specifies the identity morphisms of the corresponding category C . We can interpret the polybox picture for like so:

![[Polynomial Functors_ A Mathematical Theory of Interaction.assets/figure-0247.png]]

Here we have given the label idy to the arrow sending objects to their identity morphisms.

Keep in mind that from the domain-centric polynomial perspective, we have not yet specified that the codomain of an identity morphism is equal to its domain; that comes later.

## The right erasure law on positions: some bookkeeping

Keeping our label idy for the arrow in , the right erasure law # ( ⊳ ) = id from (7.15) can be drawn in polyboxes like so:

![[Polynomial Functors_ A Mathematical Theory of Interaction.assets/figure-0248.png]]

We have not fi lled all the boxes, but we have fi lled enough to interpret the right erasure law on positions: that the bottom arrow of the duplicator must be the identity function on ( 1 ) . Equipped with this knowledge, we can focus on the other two arrows of .

## The duplicator assigns codomains and composites

In fact, in the polybox picture for : → ⊳ , the middle arrow specifies codomains, and the top arrow specifies composition. We therefore label these arrows as follows: 4

![[Polynomial Functors_ A Mathematical Theory of Interaction.assets/figure-0249.png]]

To check that this makes sense, we fi ll in the boxes:

![[Polynomial Functors_ A Mathematical Theory of Interaction.assets/figure-0250.png]]

Remember: each position box contains an object of C , while each direction box contains a morphism of C emanating from the object below. So takes an object ∈ Ob C and a morphism : → \_ in C and assigns another object cod ∈ Ob C to be the codomain of . It then takes another morphism : cod → \_ in C and assigns a morphism # : → \_ to be the composite of and . In this way, every morphism gets a codomain, and every pair of morphisms that can be composed (i.e. the codomain of one matches the domain of the other) is assigned a composite. As with the identity morphism, we don't know what the codomain of this composite morphism is yet; but we do know that the domain of # matches the domain of , as it should.

4 Compare these labels to the names 'target' and 'run' that we gave to the arrows of a state system's transition lens.

## The left erasure law on positions: codomains of identities

As with the right erasure law, we can partially fill in the polyboxes for the left erasure law 𝛿 ( 𝜖 ⊳ 𝔠 ) = id 𝔠 from (7.15) to read what it says on positions:

![[Polynomial Functors_ A Mathematical Theory of Interaction.assets/figure-0251.png]]

So the left erasure law on positions guarantees that cod id 𝑖 = 𝑖 for all 𝑖 ∈ Ob C . It makes sense that we would find this here: the eraser assigns identities, while the duplicator assigns codomains, so a statement about codomains of identities is a coherence condition between the eraser and the duplicator.

## The erasure laws on directions are the identity laws

Let us finish filling in the polyboxes for the left and right erasure laws to see what they have to say on directions. In the picture below, the left equality depicts the left erasure law (to conserve space, we'll substitute 𝑖 for cod id 𝑖 on the left, which we now know we can do), while the right equality depicts the right erasure law:

![[Polynomial Functors_ A Mathematical Theory of Interaction.assets/figure-0252.png]]

𝔠

We fi nd that on directions, the erasure laws state that for every object ∈ Ob C and morphism : → \_ in C ,

![[Polynomial Functors_ A Mathematical Theory of Interaction.assets/formula-0558.png]]

But these are precisely the identity laws of the category C .

## The coassociative law on positions: codomains of composites

It remains to consider the comonoid's coassociative law (7.16), # ( ⊳ ) = # ( ⊳ ) . To read what it says on positions, we draw the polyboxes and fill them in, stopping just short of the uppermost direction box of the codomain:

![[Polynomial Functors_ A Mathematical Theory of Interaction.assets/figure-0253.png]]

So on positions, the coassociative law states that given an object ∈ Ob C and morphisms : → \_ and : cod → \_ in C ,

![[Polynomial Functors_ A Mathematical Theory of Interaction.assets/formula-0559.png]]

Hence composites are assigned the proper codomains.

## The coassociative law on directions is the associative law of composition

Finally, let us fi ll in the remaining polyboxes for the coassociative law (we'll substitute cod for cod ( # ) on the left, which we now know we can do):

![[Polynomial Functors_ A Mathematical Theory of Interaction.assets/figure-0254.png]]

Thus, on directions, the coassociative law states that given an object ∈ Ob C and morphisms : → \_ , : cod → \_ , and ℎ : cod → \_ in C ,

![[Polynomial Functors_ A Mathematical Theory of Interaction.assets/formula-0560.png]]

But this is precisely the associative law of composition in a category.

We've seen that the data and equations of polynomial comonoids correspond exactly to the data and equations of categories. This proves Theorem 7.28.

## Generalized duplicators as unbiased composition

Before we move onto examples, one more note about the theory: notice that both sides of our coassociative law are given by ( 3 ) : → ⊳ 3 , as defined in Proposition 7.20. On directions, ( 3 ) tells us how to compose ℎ # # ℎ three morphisms → -\_ → -\_ → -\_ in C all at once to obtain - - - - -→ \_, and (co)associativity ensures this is well-defined.

In general, ( ) : → ⊳ on directions tells us how to compose morphisms in C for each ∈ N . After all, we have already seen that 2 ( ) = performs binary composition, that ( 1 ) = id performs 'unary' composition (the 'unary composite' of a single morphism is just itself), and that ( 0 ) = performs 'nullary' composition (the 'nullary composite' at any object is just its identity). The directions of ⊳ at positions in the image of ( ) are exactly the sequences of composable morphisms of length , and ( ) sends each sequence to the single direction that is its composite.

## 7.2.2 Examples of categories as comonoids

Now that we know that polynomial comonoids are just categories, let's review some simple examples of categories and see how they may be interpreted as comonoids. As we go through these examples, pay attention to how the polynomial perspective causes us to view these familiar categories somewhat differently than usual.

## Preorders

A preorder (or thin category ) is a category in which every morphism : → is the only morphism → . 5 Composition in preorders is easy to describe, because the composite of → and → is always just the unique arrow → . As such, preorders are some of the simplest examples of categories to consider-we already saw several in Exercise 7.33-so let us interpret these as comonoids fi rst.

Example 7.34 . Let us revisit Example 7.23, where we first wrote down a comonoid that was not a state system. We defined B { } { id , } +{ } y { id y }  2 y + y and gave it a comonoid structure, with eraser : → y specifying directions id and id and duplicator : → ⊳ pointing the direction at .

Looking at the picture we drew of the comonoid in (7.24), it should come as no surprise that the corresponding category A is the walking arrow category , which is a preorder with two objects and one morphism between them:

![[Polynomial Functors_ A Mathematical Theory of Interaction.assets/formula-0561.png]]

Here we omit the identity morphisms from our picture, but we know that they exist.

The category A has two objects, the -positions and . It has two morphisms with domain , the [ ] -directions id and ; and one morphism with domain , the [ ] -direction id . The mor- phisms id and id picked out by the erasure are the identity morphisms, and the duplicator assigns them codomains that are equal to their domains. The duplicator also assigns the codomain ; and as A is then a preorder, composites are determined automatically.

5 Sometimes these are also called posets , short for partially ordered sets , but strictly speaking the only isomorphisms in a poset are its identities, while a preorder allows objects to be isomorphic without being equal.

Example 7.38 (State systems as categories) . We know that every state system  y with its do-nothing section : → y and its transition lens : → ⊳ is a comonoid, so what category S does ( , , ) correspond to?

Recall from Example 7.22 that state systems are exactly those comonoids whose codomain (i.e. 'target') functions cod: [ ] → ( 1 ) for ∈ ( 1 ) are bijections. That is, from every object ∈ Ob S = ( 1 ) , there is exactly 1 morphism to every object ∈ Ob S . So not only is S a preorder, it is the codiscrete preorder on ( 1 ) , where there

-

→

is always a morphism between every pair of objects.

Let us redraw the polyboxes for the do-nothing section of from (7.1) and the transition lens of from (7.4), this time with our new arrow labels, as a sanity check:

![[Polynomial Functors_ A Mathematical Theory of Interaction.assets/figure-0255.png]]

Indeed, we had already been writing the directions of as arrows → , knowing that each was uniquely specified by its source and its target in ( 1 ) . And in Section 7.1.3, we had already noted that id was just the arrow → . So state systems have been categories with exactly one morphism between every pair of objects all along.

Other names for this category include the indiscrete preorder and the codiscrete or indiscrete category . These names highlight the fact that every object of this category is isomorphic to every other object: in fact, every arrow → is an isomorphism with inverse → , for these compose as id : → in one direction and id : → in the other. Thus this category is also a groupoid , and it may be called the codiscrete , indiscrete , or contractible groupoid . . . but we will call it the state category on , where is the set of positions of or objects of S .

Here are the state categories on 3 and on 15 , with all maps (even The picture on the left should look familiar: it is what we drew in Example 7.6 when took the corolla picture for 3 3 y and bent the arrows to point at their targets according to its transition lens. Notice that the graphs we obtain in this way are always complete.

![[Polynomial Functors_ A Mathematical Theory of Interaction.assets/figure-0256.png]]

Exercise 7.39 . Let be a set. Is there any comonoid structure on y other than that of the state category?

Not only does Example 7.38 fi nally explain what our state systems really are (they're just special categories!), it illustrates two important features of our story. One is that on positions, the duplicator : → ⊳ of a comonoid takes the corolla picture of and 'bends the arrows' so that they point to other roots, yielding the underlying graph of a category. Then on directions collapses two-arrow paths in the graph down to individual arrows, while the eraser : → y identifies empty paths with identity arrows.

Another important point is that we can view any category as a generalized state system : its objects as states , and its morphisms as transitions between states. The polynomial comonoid perspective is particularly suited for thinking about categories in this way: each object is a position that we could be in, and each morphism out of that object is a direction that we might take. What is special about a comonoid is that each direction will always have another position at the end of it, making it reasonable to think of these directions as transitions between different states; and any sequence of transitions that we can follow is itself a transition.

Comparing these ideas, we see that they say the same thing: the first from the perspective of trees and graphs, the second from the perspective of dynamics. We might say that

a comonoid structure on a corolla forest turns roots into vertices and leaves into composable arrows between vertices;

or that a comonoid structure on a polynomial turns positions into states and directions into composable transitions between states.

## Monoids and monoid actions

Here we use monoid to refer to a monoid in the monoidal category ( Set , 1 , ×) . We denote such a monoid by ( , , ∗) , where is the underlying set, ∈ is the unit, and ∗ : × → is the binary operation.

Example 7.40 (Monoids as representable comonoids) . Recall that every monoid ( , , ∗) can be identified with a 1-object category M with a single hom-set , a single identity morphism , and composition given by ∗ . Now we know that a 1-object category M is also a polynomial comonoid ( , , ) whose carrier has 1 position, with all of the morphisms of M becoming its directions. So the carrier of M is the representable polynomial y .

Then the eraser : y → y picks out the identity morphism ∈ on directions, while the duplicator : y → y ⊳ y  × y can be identified with the binary operation ∗ : × → . (We don't have to worry about codomains, since there is only one possi- ble codomain to choose from.) In this way, every monoid ( , , ∗) in Set gives rise to a representable comonoid ( y , , ) in Poly . We can just as easily invert this construction, obtaining a monoid for every representable comonoid by taking the underlying set to be the carrier's set of directions, the unit to be the direction picked out by the erasure, and the binary operation to be the duplicator's on-directions function.

Exercise 7.41 . Verify Example 7.40 by showing that ( , , ∗) satisfies the unitality and associativity requirements of a monoid in ( Set , 1 , ×) if and only if ( y , , ) satisfies the erasure and coassociativity requirements of a comonoid in ( Poly , y , ⊳ ) .

Example 7.42 (Cyclic lists) . For any ∈ N , consider Z / Z , the cyclic group of order , viewed as a monoid or, equivalently, a 1-object category. Its carrier is Z y / Z .

As a polynomial functor, Z y / Z sends each set to the set of lengthtuples in . But the comonoid structure lets us think of these tuples as cyclic lists : once we reach the last element, we can loop back around to the fi rst element. Indeed, as a natural transformation, : Z y / Z → y picks out the 'current' element via its -component ⊳ : Z y / Z ⊳ → y ⊳ , which is just a function : Z / Z → ; and lets us move around the list.

We will see later that comonoids are closed under coproducts, Í so ∈ N y Z / Z is also a comonoid.

Example 7.43 (Monoid actions) . Suppose that ( , , ∗) is a monoid, is a set, and : × → is a (right) monoid action . That is, for all ∈ we have ( , ) = and ( , ∗ ) = ( ( , ) , ) for

, ∈ ; equivalently, the diagrams

![[Polynomial Functors_ A Mathematical Theory of Interaction.assets/figure-0257.png]]

commute.

Then there is an associated category MA with objects in and morphisms - → ( , ) for each ∈ and ∈ . This, in turn, corresponds to a comonoid ( y , , ) , as we will see in the next exercise.

Example 7.45 (The category of -streams) . Fix a set . The set N consists of countable sequences of elements in , which we will call -streams . We can write a -stream ∈ N as

with ∈ for each ∈ N .

Then there is a monoid action 𝜏 : 𝐵 N × N → 𝐵 N for which

Roughly speaking, N acts on -streams by shifting them forward by a natural number of steps. We can check that this is a monoid action by observing that ( , 0 ) = and that ( , + ) = ( ( , ) , ) .

So by Example 7.43, the corresponding comonoid is carried by N N y . Each -stream is a position, and each ∈ N is a direction at that can be visualized as the sequence of arrows starting from 0 and ending at . Then at the end of the direction is a new -stream: the rest of starting at . Indeed, this -stream is exactly ( , ) , the codomain assigned to the direction at .

Alternatively, if we shift from the domain-centric perspective to the usual hom-set perspective, this comonoid corresponds to a category whose objects are -streams and whose morphisms → ′ consist of every way in which ′ can be viewed as a continguous substream of : that is, there is a morphism : → ′ for each ∈ N satisfying

![[Polynomial Functors_ A Mathematical Theory of Interaction.assets/formula-0564.png]]

The identity on is given by 0: → ; and the composite of two morphisms is the sum of the corresponding natural numbers, as a substream of a substream of is just a substream of shifted by the appropriate amount.

We will see this category again in Example 8.38.

## The degree of an object

We could continue to list examples of polynomial comonoids, but of course any list of small categories is already a list of such comonoids. So instead, we conclude this section with some terminology that the polynomial perspective on a category affords.

Definition 7.47 (Degree, linear) . Let C be a category and ∈ Ob C an object. The degree of , denoted deg ( ) , is the set of arrows in C that emanate from .

If deg ( )  1 , we say that is linear . If deg ( )  n for ∈ N , we say has degree .

## 7.3 Morphisms of polynomial comonoids are retrofunctors

Now that we have characterized the comonoids of Poly , let us consider the morphisms between them. These turn out to correspond to a rather odd kind of map between categories known as a retrofunctor . 6

6 Many authors have referred to these as cofunctors , including ourselves in other work and in early versions of this book. However, the prefix co in category theory is very special-having to do with taking opposites-and we will see in Remark 7.59 that comonoid homomorphisms are not just opposite-functors. Thus to keep the prefix co more pristine, and in solidarity with other researchers, we have decided to use the term retrofunctor , which is an appropriate usage of the term defined by Bob Paré [Par23].

## 7.3.1 Introducing comonoid morphisms and retrofunctors

First, let us define morphisms of comonoids in the most general setting. If you've seen the definition of a monoid homomorphism (or even a group homomorphism), then this definition may look familiar.

Definition 7.49 (Comonoid morphism) . Given a monoidal category ( C , y , ⊳ ) with comonoids C B ( , , ) and C ′ B ( ′ , ′ , ′ ) , a comonoid morphism (or morphism of comonoids ) C → C ′ is a morphism : → ′ in C for which the following diagrams commute:

![[Polynomial Functors_ A Mathematical Theory of Interaction.assets/formula-0565.png]]

called the eraser preservation law (we say preserves erasure ); and

![[Polynomial Functors_ A Mathematical Theory of Interaction.assets/formula-0566.png]]

called the duplicator preservation law (we say preserves duplication ). We may also say that preserves the comonoid structure .

When the monoidal structure on C can be inferred, we let Comon ( C ) denote the subcategory of C whose objects are comonoids in C and whose morphisms are comonoid morphisms.

So when our monoidal category of interest is ( Poly , y , ⊳ ) , a morphism between polynomial comonoids is just a special kind of lens between their carriers that preserves erasure and duplication.

Exercise 7.52 . There is something to be proved in Definition 7.49: that comonoids and comonoid morphisms really do form a category. Using the notation from Definition 7.49, verify the following:

2. The composite of two comonoid morphisms is a comonoid morphism.

This will show that Comon ( C ) is indeed a subcategory of C .

Notice that we were very careful in how we stated Theorem 7.28: while we asserted the existence of an isomorphism-preserving one-toone correspondence between the objects of Comon ( Poly ) and Cat , we never claimed that these two categories are isomorphic or even equivalent. The strange truth of the matter is that they are not: polynomial comonoid morphisms correspond not to functors, but to different maps of categories called retrofunctors .

How exactly do these maps behave? If we specify Definition 7.49 to the case of ( Poly , y , ⊳ ) , we can write the eraser preservation law (7.50) in polyboxes as

![[Polynomial Functors_ A Mathematical Theory of Interaction.assets/figure-0258.png]]

and the duplicator preservation law (7.51) in polyboxes as

![[Polynomial Functors_ A Mathematical Theory of Interaction.assets/figure-0259.png]]

If we read off the equations from these polyboxes, interpreting polynomial comonoids as categories, we derive the following definition of a retrofunctor. (Here (7.56) is equivalent to (7.53), while (7.57) and (7.58) are together equivalent to (7.54).)

Definition 7.55 (Retrofunctor) . Let C and C ′ be (small) categories. A : ↛ ′ consists of

retrofunctor C C

- a function : Ob C → Ob C ′ forward on objects 7 and

- a function ♯ : C ′ [ ] → C [ ] backward on morphisms for each ∈ Ob C ,

satisfying the following conditions, collectively known as the retrofunctor laws :

- i. preserves identities :

for each ∈ Ob C ;

- ii. preserves codomains :

- iii. preserves composites : 8

![[Polynomial Functors_ A Mathematical Theory of Interaction.assets/formula-0569.png]]

We let Cat ♯  Comon ( Poly ) denote the category of (small) categories and retrofunctors.

Since each retrofunctor includes a lens between its carrier polynomials, retrofunctors compose the way lenses do.

Exercise 7.60 . Let C , D , E be categories, and let : C ↛ D and : D ↛ E be retrofunctors between them.

1. Characterize the behavior of the identity retrofunctor id D on D . Where does it send each object? Where does it send each morphism?
2. Characterize the behavior of the composite retrofunctor # . Where does it send each object and morphism?

On the surface, functors and retrofunctors have much in common: both send objects to objects and morphisms to morphisms in a way that preserves domains and codomains as well as identities and composites. The main difference is that functors send morphisms forward , while retrofunctors send morphisms backward . As we work with retrofunctors, it will be helpful to remember the following:

9 As a hint to the connection in terms of the (yet-undefined) double category C at ♯ , note that when the monads in question are left adjoints, their right adjoints will automatically be comonads, and a morphism between these comonads will be a retromorphism between the monads.

A retrofunctor goes forward on objects and backward on morphisms. Codomains are objects, so preserves them going forward. Identities and composites are morphisms, so preserves them going

backward.

Before we explore just how different functors and retrofunctors can be, let us note a few more similarities that these two kinds of maps between categories share. For example, retrofunctors, like functors, preserve isomorphisms.

Proposition 7.61. Let : C ↛ D be a retrofunctor, ∈ C be an object, and : → \_ be an isomorphism in D . Then ♯ is also an isomorphism in C .

Proof. Let ′ B cod ♯ , so that ′ = cod by (7.57), and let -1 : ′ → be the inverse of . Then

![[Polynomial Functors_ A Mathematical Theory of Interaction.assets/formula-0570.png]]

so in particular = cod id = cod ♯ � -1  , and ′

![[Polynomial Functors_ A Mathematical Theory of Interaction.assets/formula-0571.png]]

♯ �  Hence and ♯ -1 ′ are inverses, and the result follows. □

Moreover, isomorphisms in Cat correspond to isomorphisms in Cat ♯ .

Exercise 7.62 . We've justified the 'isomorphism-preserving' part of Theorem 7.28 implicitly, but let's make it explicit.

Recall that two categories C and D are isomorphic in Cat if there exist functors : C → D and : D → C that are mutually inverse, i.e. # and # are identity functors on C and D . Similarly, C and D are isomorphic in Cat ♯ if there exist retrofunctors : C ↛ D and : D ↛ C that are mutually inverse, i.e. # and # are identity retrofunctors on C and D . Show that C and D are isomorphic in Cat if and only if they are isomorphic in Cat ♯ .

But while isomorphisms in Cat ♯ are the same as those in Cat , the nonisomorphisms can be very different. We will see this in the examples to come.

## 7.3.2 Examples of retrofunctors

From a realm where functors reign supreme, the back-and-forth behavior of retrofunctors can seem foreign and counterintuitive. Whereas a functor C → D can be thought of as a diagram -a picture in the shape of C , drawn with the objects and arrows of D -retrofunctors are much more like the dynamical systems of Chapter 4. 10

That is, a retrofunctor : C ↛ D is a way of interacting with the states (objects) and transitions (morphisms) within C by way of D . Imagine the retrofunctor as a box, with C on the inside and D on the outside. Some ∈ C may be the current state inside the box, but all anyone outside the box can see is the object ∈ D that the box chooses to display in lieu of . Still, any transition out of can be selected from the outside; the box guarantees that whatever is on the inside, there is a corresponding transition ♯ out of that . As is followed from to cod on the outside, ♯ is followed from to cod ♯ on the inside. But codomain preservation guarantees that the new state cod on the outside is equal to what the box would want to display in lieu of the new state cod ♯ on the inside, as cod = cod ♯ , Then the process repeats in a manner compatible with identities and composition.

10 In fact, we will see in Chapter 8 that retrofunctors generalize our dynamical systems.

Here we give a variety of examples of retrofunctors to get a better handle on them. Often we will denote a category by its carrier when its comonoid structure can be inferred from context, and C will be a category throughout with carrier .

## Retrofunctors to preorders

Given a retrofunctor from C to a preorder P , we can think of P as providing a simplified model or abstraction of the states and transitions possible in C , picking canonical transitions in C along the way to exhibit the model. While the transitions in a general category may be more complex, all that a preorder tells you is whether you can get from one state to another or not. Let's see some examples.

Example 7.63 (Retrofunctors to discrete categories) . The discrete category on a set is the category with objects in and only identity morphisms; its carrier is y . So a retrofunctor : C ↛ y is completely determined by its behavior on objects: to preserve identities, it can only send the morphisms in y back to the identity morphisms in C . We can identify with a function Ob C → , assigning each state in C a label in without revealing anything about the transitions between them.

## Retrofunctors to monoids

When a monoid ( , , ∗) , viewed as a 1-object category y , is the codomain of a retrofunctor C ↛ y , it plays the role of a joystick: an 'input device' that 'reports its . . . direction to the device it is controlling.' 11 Like a joystick, y stays in one 'place'-a single state-but has a number of directions it can take that are reported back to C , controlling the way it moves through its transitions. As we string together a sequence of directions in , we chart a course through the transitions of C . We make this analogy concrete in the following examples.

Example 7.71 (Arrow fi elds) . Consider the monoid ( N , 0 , +) viewed as a category N y . Retrofunctors N C ↛ y have been called admissible sections [Agu97]. We prefer to call them arrow fi elds (on C ), for they turn out to resemble vector fi elds-but with arrows in C instead of vectors. 12 We'll have more to say about these in Theorem 8.61, but our goal here is simply to unpack the definition.

To specify a retrofunctor : C ↛ N y , we fi rst say what it does on objects, but this is already decided: there is only one object in N y , so every object of C is sent to it. This also means that codomains are automatically preserved. So as will be the case for all retrofunctors to monoids, is characterized by its behavior on morphisms: for each object ∈ C , the retrofunctor assigns each ∈ N a morphism ♯ of C emanating from . That is a large amount of data, but we still have two retrofunctor laws to pare it down:

11 Description from Wikipedia.

![[Polynomial Functors_ A Mathematical Theory of Interaction.assets/formula-0574.png]]

Then for each ∈ C , since every ∈ N is a sum of 1's, the morphism ♯ can decomposed be into copies of ♯ 1 for objects 0 B , 1 , . . . , ∈ C , as follows:

![[Polynomial Functors_ A Mathematical Theory of Interaction.assets/formula-0575.png]]

Here each + 1 B cod ♯ 1.

Thus an arrow fi eld of C is given by independently choosing a morphism emanting from each ∈ C to be ♯ 1: an arrow (morphism) out of each object, like how a vector fi eld has a vector out of each point. Indeed, any such choice uniquely determines the retrofunctor : C ↛ y N : as every object is assigned an arrow coming out of it, we can follow the arrow out of to an object 1 , then following the arrow out of 1 to an object 2 , and so on until we have followed the arrows in (7.72), which then compose to yield ♯ . With our joystick analogy, a single fl ick sends us from a state ∈ C along its assigned arrow ♯ 1, while fl icks send us through arrows along the arrow fi eld.

Here is an example of an arrow fi eld, depicted in solid red, on the product of preorders ( N , ≤) × ( N , ≤) , depicted in dashed black arrows:

![[Polynomial Functors_ A Mathematical Theory of Interaction.assets/figure-0265.png]]

Every object has been assigned an emanating morphism drawn in solid red, but there need not be any rhyme or reason to our choice.

Proposition 7.79. There is a fully faithful functor Mon op Cat ♯ → , whose image consists of all categories whose carriers are representable.

Proof. Given a monoid ( , , ∗) , we think of it as a category with one object; its carrier y is representable. A retrofunctor between such categories carries no data in its on-objects part, and codomains are automatically preserved. Retrofunctors y → y simply carry elements of to elements of , preserving identity and composition, exactly the description of monoid homomorphisms. □

Proposition 7.80. There is an adjunction

Cat ♯ ( C , y )  Set ( Ob C , )

for C ∈ Cat ♯ and ∈ Set .

Proof. In the solution to Exercise 7.36, we saw that a category is discrete iff its carrier is a linear polynomial: this occurs when the only arrow emanating from each object is its identity. Thus y corresponds to a discrete category. A retrofunctor from any category to a discrete category needs to say what happens on objects, but the rest of the data is determined because identities need to be sent back to identities. This is the content of the proposition. □

## Retrofunctors from state categories

By now we should be very familiar with lenses from state categories, which are our original dynamical systems. A retrofunctor from a state category, then, is just a dynamical system that satisfies the retrofunctor laws. It turns out that retrofunctors from state categories are particularly noteworthy: just as a polynomial comonoid C can be identified with a category, a retrofunctor out of C can be identified with a number of equivalent categorical constructions on C , perhaps the most familiar being a functor C → Set . But these equivalences deserve their own subsection to examine in full; we'll defer them to Section 7.3.3. For now, let's look at some examples of retrofunctors out of state categories.

Example 7.84 (Retrofunctors y ↛ C are C -coalgebras) . Recall from Example 6.66 that for a set , lenses y → correspond to functions → ( ) known as coalgebras for the functor . As a retrofunctor y ↛ C is just a special kind of lens from y to , the carrier of C , it should correspond to a special kind of coalgebra → ( ) for the functor .

Taking = = ∈ Set in (6.64), we fi nd that there is a natural isomorphism between dynamical systems y → and functions → ( ) , also known as a coalgebra for the functor or a -coalgebra . 13

Example 7.85 (Retrofunctors between state categories are very well-behaved lenses) . Our familiar state category on from Example 7.38 is the category with objects in and exactly 1 morphism between every pair of objects; when we label each morphism with its codomain, its carrier is y , the identity of ∈ is , and (disregarding domains) ′ = ′ for composable , ′ ∈ .

# Then a retrofunctor y ↛ y between two state categories corresponds to what is known to functional programmers as a very well-behaved lens . We actually defined this way back in Example 3.43, where we called the on-objects (on-positions) function of such a retrofunctor get: → , and the on-morphisms (on-directions) function put: × → . 14 Then the retrofunctor laws are as follows:

1. Preservation of identities (7.56) becomes

![[Polynomial Functors_ A Mathematical Theory of Interaction.assets/formula-0579.png]]

for all ∈ , known as the get-put law (named in diagrammatic order: we apply get before we apply put).

2. Preservation of codomains (7.57) becomes

![[Polynomial Functors_ A Mathematical Theory of Interaction.assets/formula-0580.png]]

for all ∈ and ∈ , known as the put-get law .

13 There are two versions of coalgebras we are interested in (and more that we are not) with distinct definitions: a coalgebra for a functor , which is the version used here, and a coalgebra for a comonad , which is a coalgebra for a functor with extra conditions that we will introduce later.

## 3. Preservation of composition becomes

![[Polynomial Functors_ A Mathematical Theory of Interaction.assets/formula-0581.png]]

for all ∈ and , ′ ∈ , known as the put-put law .

In fact, it turns out that these laws can be satisfied if and only if get is a product projection! For example, if the cardinalities | | and | | of and are fi nite and | | is not divisible by | | , then there are no retrofunctors y ↛ y . A stringent condition, no? We'll explore it in Exercise 7.87 below.

## Other retrofunctors

Example 7.90 (Objects aren't representable in Cat ♯ ) . In the world of categories and the usual functors between them, the terminal category T B · with one object and one morphism represents objects , in the sense that functors T → C naturally correspond to objects in C .

Unfortunately, the same cannot be said for retrofunctors: we'll see in Exercise 7.91 that there does not exist a fi xed category U for which retrofunctors U ↛ C are in bijection with objects in C for every category C .

Retrofunctors T ↛ C are somewhat strange beasts: because they must preserve codomains, they can be identified with objects ∈ C for which the codomain of every emanating morphism → ′ is ′ = itself.

## 7.3.3 Equivalent characterizations of retrofunctors from state categories

Fix a category C throughout with polynomial carrier . How can we view the data of a retrofunctor from a state category y ↛ C ? This is actually a very natural categorical concept-we'll see some equivalent ways to express this data below, then state and prove even more equivalences next chapter when we have the machinery to do so.

## As coalgebras

Recall from Example 6.66 that for a set , lenses y → correspond to functions → ( ) known as coalgebras for the functor . As a retrofunctor y ↛ C is just a special kind of lens from y to , it should correspond to a special kind of coalgebra → ( ) for the functor . Indeed, whenever carries a comonoid C with respect to the composition product (i.e. a comonad), there is a special notion of a C -coalgebra (i.e. a coalgebra for the comonad C ), as follows.

## As discrete opfibrations

The concept of a C -coalgebra is in turn equivalent to a better known categorical construction on C , which we introduce here.

Definition 7.99 (Discrete opfibration) . Let C be a category. A pair ( S , ) , where S is a category and : S → C is a functor, is called a discrete opfibration over C if it satisfies the following condition:

- for every object ∈ S , object ′ ∈ C , and morphism : ( ) → ′ there exists a unique object ′ ∈ S and morphism : → ′ such that ( ) = . Note that in this case ( ′ ) = .

A morphism of discrete opfibrations ( S , ) → ( S ′ , ′ ) over C is a functor : S → S ′ making the following triangle commute: We refer to as the lift of to .

A morphism , ′ ( S ) → ( S , ′ ) between discrete opfibrations over C is a functor : S → S ′ making the following triangle commute:

![[Polynomial Functors_ A Mathematical Theory of Interaction.assets/figure-0277.png]]

We denote the category of discrete opfibrations over C by dopf ( C ) .

## As copresheaves

It is well-known in the category theory literature that the category of discrete opfibrations over C is equivalent to yet another familiar category: the category Set C , whose objects are functors C → Set . Such a functor is known as a copresheaf on C for short. These are relevant, e.g. in the theory of categorical databases [Spi12]. Here we review what is needed to understand this equivalence. We begin by giving a standard construction on any copresheaf.

Definition 7.104 (Category of elements) . Given a copresheaf on C , ∫ i.e. a functor : Set , its category of elements C C → is defined to have objects

The category is so named because its objects are the elements of the sets that the objects of C are sent to by . Each morphism ∫ : → \_ in C then becomes as many morphisms in C as there are elements of , tracking where sends each such element.

The next exercise shows how this construction turns every copresheaf into a discrete opfibration.

To summarize, we have four equivalent notions:

- (1) retrofunctors : y ↛ C ;
- (2) C -coalgebras ( , ) , with : → ⊳ ;
- (3) discrete opfibrations : S → C , with Ob S = ;
4. ∫ copresheaves : C C → Set , with Ob = . (4)

Moreover, (2), (3), and (4) form equivalent categories. Translating between these notions yields different perspectives on familiar categorical concepts. In the next chapter, we will discover even more characterizations of the same data within Poly (see Proposition 8.68).

## 7.4 Summary and further reading

In this chapter we began by showing that for every set , the thing that makes a dynamical system like y → actually run is the fact that y has the structure of a comonoid. We then explained Ahmanand-Uustalu's result that comonoids, i.e. polynomials , equipped with a pair of lenses : → y and : → ⊳ , are exactly categories [AU16]. We explained how picks out an identity for each object and how picks out a codomain for each morphism and a composite for each composable pair of morphisms. In particular we showed that the category corresponding to y is the contractible groupoid on , i.e. the category with -many objects and a unique morphism between any two.

We then discussed how comonoid morphisms → are not functors but retrofunctors : they map forwards on objects and backwards on morphisms. Retrofunctors were fi rst defined by Marcelo Aguiar [Agu97], though his definition was opposite to ours; he referred to these as cofunctors . Retrofunctors are the morphisms of a category we notate as Cat ♯ . We showed that retrofunctors between state categories ↛ y y are what are known in the functional programming community as very well-behaved lenses . For more on lenses, see [nLa22].

## Solution to Exercise 7.36.

The linear polynomial y corresponds to a category whose objects form the set and whose only morphisms are identities: in other words, it is the discrete category on .

## Solution to Exercise 7.37.

1. The polynomial B y n + 1 + n y has + 1 positions: 1 with + 1 directions and the rest with 1 direction each. So any category carried by has + 1 objects, of which only 1 has any nonidentity morphisms coming out of it: in fact, it has nonidentity morphisms coming out of it. But if the category is to be a preorder, each of these nonidentity morphisms must have a distinct codomain. As there are exactly other objects, this completely characterizes the category. Equivalently, it is the discrete category on n adjoined with a unique initial object, so that the only nonidentity morphisms are the morphisms out of that initial object to each of the other objects exactly once.
2. This category can be thought of as 'star-shaped' if we picture the initial object in the center with morphisms leading out to the other objects like spokes.

## Solution to Exercise 7.39.

In the case of B 0 , the only comonoid structure on y  0 is given by the empty category, the only category with no objects; and in the case of B 1 , the only comonoid structure on y  y is given by the category with 1 object and no nonidentity morphisms, again the only such category. So in those cases, the comonoid structure on y is unique.

Now assume | | ≥ 2. The state category is always connected, but we can always fi nd a comonoid structure on y given by a category that is not connected-and thus not isomorphic to the state category-as follows. Consider a category whose object set is that has no morphisms between distinct objects, so that it is certainly not connected. Then to specify the category, it suffices to specify a monoid associated with each object that will give the morphisms whose domain and codomain are equal to that object.

But there is always a monoid structure on a given nonempty set . If is fi nite, we can take the cyclic group Z /| | Z of order | | , so that the resulting category has carrier Z /| | Z  y y . On the other hand, if is infinite, we can take the free monoid on , Í which has cardinality | | n = | N | | | N | ∈ = | . So the resulting category will again have carrier y .

## Solution to Exercise 7.48.

1. If every object in C is linear, then the only morphisms in C are the identity morphisms, so C must be a discrete category.
2. It is not possible for an object in C to have degree 0, as every object must have at least an identity morphism emanating from it.
3. Some possible examples of categories with objects of degree N are the monoid ( N , 0 , +) (see Exercise 7.33 #4), the poset ( N , ≤) (see Exercise 7.33 #5), and the state category on N (see Example 7.38).
4. Up to isomorphism, there are 3 categories with just one linear and one quadratic object. They can be distinguished by the behavior of the single nonidentity morphism. Either its domain and its codomain are distinct, in which case we have the walking arrow category; or its domain and its codomain are the same, in which case it can be composed with itself to obtain either itself or the identity. So there are 3 possible categories in total.
5. Yes: since (isomorphic) categories correspond to (isomorphic) comonoids, there are as many categories with one linear and one quadratic object up to isomorphism as there are comonoid structures on y 2 + y .

## Solution to Exercise 7.62.

We want to show that categories C and D are isomorphic in Cat if and only if they are isomorphic in Cat ♯ . First, assume that C and D are isomorphic in Cat , so that there exist mutually inverse functors : C → D and : D → C . Then we can define a retrofunctor : C ↛ D such that for each ∈ C we have B ∈ D , and for each ∈ D [ ] we have ♯ B ∈ [ C ] = C [ ] . We can verify that really is a retrofunctor: it preserves identities and composition because does, and it preserves codomains because cod ♯ = cod = cod = cod . Analogously, we can define a retrofunctor : D ↛ C with B and ♯ B for each ∈ D and ∈ C [ ] . Then # is equal to # both on objects and on morphisms, so it is the identity retrofunctor on C ; analogously, # is the identity retrofunctor on D . Thus C and D are isomorphic in Cat ♯ .

Conversely, assume C and D are isomorphic in Cat ♯ , so that there exist mutually inverse retrofunctors : C ↛ D and : D ↛ C . Given objects , ′ and a morphism : ′ in , we have that → C = , so ♯ is a function C [ ] → D [ ] . In particular, ♯ is a morphism in D whose domain is and whose codomain satisfies cod ♯ = cod = ′ , and thus cod ♯ = ′ . Hence we can define a functor : C → D such that for each ∈ C we have B ∈ D , and for each morphism

: → ′ in C we have B ♯ : → ′ . Functoriality follows from the fact that preserves identities and composition. Analogously, we can define a functor : D → C with B for each ∈ D and B ♯ for each : → ′ in D . Then # is equal to # on objects; on morphisms, it sends each : → ′ in  C to    ♯ ♯ = ♯ ♯ = ( # ♯ ) = itself. So # is the identity retrofunctor on C . Analogously, # is the identity functor on D . Thus C and D are isomorphic in Cat .

## 8. Categorical properties of polynomial comonoids

While we defined the category Cat ♯ of categories and retrofunctors in the last chapter, we have only begun to scratch the surface of the properties it satisfies. As the category of comonoids in Poly , equipped with a canonical forgetful functor : Cat ♯ → Poly sending each polynomial to its carrier, Cat ♯ inherits many of the categorical properties satisfied by Poly . Underpinning this inheritance is the fact that has a right adjoint, a cofree functor Poly → Cat ♯ . We will introduce this adjunction in the first section of this chapter. Then we will discuss how many of the categorical properties of Poly , including much of what we covered in Chapter 5, play nicely with restriction to Cat ♯ . Finally, we will touch on other constructions we can make over the comonoids in Poly , such as their comodules and coalgebras.

## 8.1 Cofree comonoids

Consider a dynamical system : → with state system and interface . In Section 7.1.5, we posed the question of whether there was a single morphism that could capture all the information encoded by the family of lenses Run ( ) : → ⊳ for all ∈ N , defined as the composite

![[Polynomial Functors_ A Mathematical Theory of Interaction.assets/formula-0617.png]]

that models runs through the system .

It turns out that there is: the key is that there is a natural way to interpret every polynomial as a category T , so that retrofunctors into T are exactly lenses into . In other words, T will turn out to be the cofree comonoid (or cofree category ) on . Cofree comonoids in Poly are beautiful objects, both in their visualizable structure as a category and in the metaphors we can make about them. They allow us to replace the interface of a dynamical system with a category and get access to a rich theory that exists there.

We'll go through the construction of the cofree comonoid and its implications in this section, featuring a purely formal proof of the fact the forgetful functor : Cat ♯ → Poly has a right adjoint T -: Poly → Cat ♯ , where for each ∈ Poly , the carrier B T of the category T is given by the limit of the following diagram:

![[Polynomial Functors_ A Mathematical Theory of Interaction.assets/figure-0294.png]]

Thus, we will show in Theorem 8.45 that and T -form a forgetfulcofree adjunction, making T the cofree comonoid on . But fi rst, let us concretely characterize the canonical comonoid structure on the limit of (8.1), before showing that it is indeed cofree.

## 8.1.1 The carrier of the cofree comonoid

Let be the limit of the diagram (8.1) in Poly ; it will turn out to be the carrier of the cofree comonoid on (where will be an arbitrary polynomial throughout). We could compute this limit directly, but we will be able to describe it more concretely in terms of what we call trees on or -trees : trees comprised of -corollas. In doing so, we will formalize the tree pictures we have been using to describe polynomials all along.

## Trees on polynomials

Definition 8.2 (Tree on a polynomial) . Let ∈ Poly be a polynomial. A tree on , or a -tree , is a rooted tree whose every vertex is assigned a -position and a bijection from the children of to [ ] . We denote the set of -trees by tree .

We can think of a -tree as being 'built' out of -corollas according to these instructions:

To choose a -tree in tree :

- its leaves in [ 0 ] will be the edges out of the root;

- its leaves in [ 1 ] will be the edges out of that vertex;

- its leaves in [ 2 ] will be the edges out of that vertex;

Of course, there may eventually be multiple copies of any one -corolla as a subgraph in our -tree, and these copies of the same -corolla should be treated as distinct. So we should really think of the positions and directions of involved each step as labels for the vertices and edges of a -tree-although crucially, each [ ] -direction must be used as a label exactly once among the edges emanating from a given vertex labeled with .

Although these instructions continue forever, we could abbreviate them by writing them recursively:

To choose a -tree in tree :

## 1. choose a -corolla:

We would like to draw some examples of -trees, but note that a -tree can have infinite height-in fact, it always will unless every one of its branches terminates at a -corolla with no leaves, i.e. a position with an empty direction set. This means that plenty of -trees cannot be drawn, even when all the position- and direction-sets of are fi nite; but we can instead consider fi nite-height portions of them that we will call pretrees .

## Pretrees on polynomials

Before we define pretrees, let us give some examples.

Example 8.3 (A few example -pretrees) . Let B { , ■ } y 2 + {·} y + { ▲ } . Here are four partially constructed -trees:

![[Polynomial Functors_ A Mathematical Theory of Interaction.assets/figure-0295.png]]

Here only the third one-the single yellow triangle ▲ -would count as an element of tree . After all, in Definition 8.2, when we speak of a tree on , we mean a tree for which every vertex is a position in with all of its emanating directions fi lled by another position in . Since three of the four trees shown in (8.4) have leaves emanating from the top that have not been fi lled by any -corollas, these trees are not elements of tree .

However, each of these trees can be extended to an actual element of tree by continually fi lling in each open leaf with another -corolla. These may continue forever, or they may terminate if every open leaf is fi lled with a direction-less yellow triangle.

Exercise 8.5 . Let B 2 + 3 1 y y . Are there any fi nite -trees? If not, could there be any vertices of a given -tree with fi nitely many descendents?

The trees in (8.4) can all be obtained by following just the first 3 levels of instructions for building a -tree (in fact, exactly as many instructions as we initially wrote out). On the other hand, we know from Section 6.1.3 that such a tree represents a position of ⊳ 3 , whose directions are its height-3 leaves-and this is true for any ∈ N in place of 3. So these trees are still important to our theory; but since they are not always complete -trees, we will call them something else.

Definition 8.6. Given ∈ Poly , a stagepretree (or ⊳ -pretree ) is defined to be an element of ⊳ ( 1 ) . For each ∈ ⊳ ( 1 ) the heightleaves of is defined to be the set ⊳ [ ] .

Remark 8.7 . In Definition 8.6, we use stage instead of height to allow for the fact that a ⊳ -pretree may not reach its maximum height if all of its branches terminate early. For example, the yellow dot in (8.4) is a ⊳ 1 -pretree, but it is also a ⊳ 2 -pretree, a ⊳ 50 -pretree, and indeed a -tree.

Note that for any polynomial ∈ Poly there is exactly one stage-0 pretree on for any ∈ Poly , because ⊳ 0 ( 1 ) = y ( 1 ) = 1 .

Example 8.8 (Trimming pretrees) . Since ⊳ 1 ( 1 )  ( 1 ) and ⊳ 0 ( 1 )  y ( 1 )  1 , the unique function ! : ( 1 ) → 1 can be thought of as a function from ⊳ 1 -positions to ⊳ 0 -positions, or equivalently a function from stage-1 pretrees (i.e. corollas) to stage-0 pretrees on . We can interpret this function as taking a corolla and 'stripping away' its leaves along with the position-label on its root, leaving only a single unlabeled root: a stage-0 pretree.

This deceptively simple function has a surprising amount of utility when combined with other maps. For any ∈ N , we can take the composition product in Poly of the identity on ⊳ and !, interpreted as a lens between constant polynomials, to obtain a lens ⊳ ⊳ ! : ⊳ ⊳ ( 1 ) → ⊳ ⊳ 1 , or equivalently a function ⊳ ( ! ) : ⊳ + 1 ( 1 ) → ⊳ ( 1 ) from stage-( + 1 ) pretrees to stage- pretrees on .

We can deduce the behavior of this function on stage-( + 1 ) pretrees from what we know about how the composition product interacts with pretrees on . The identity lens on ⊳ keeps the lower levels of each ⊳ ( + 1 ) -pretree intact, while ! will 'strip away' the ⊳ ( + 1 ) -pretree's height-( + 1 ) leaves, along with all the positionlabels on its heightvertices. Thus ⊳ ( ! ) is the function sending every stage-( + 1 ) pretree on to its stagepretree, effectively trimming it down a level.

We can go even further: composing several such functions yields a composite function

![[Polynomial Functors_ A Mathematical Theory of Interaction.assets/formula-0618.png]]

sending every stage-( + ) pretree on to its stagepretree by trimming off its top levels. We will see these functions again shortly.

## Positions of the cofree comonoid

Now recall from Example 5.36 that in Poly , the positions of a limit are the limit of the positions. Moreover, in (8.1), every vertical lens ⊳ → ⊳ ⊳ 1 is, in fact, a vertical lens in the sense of Definition 5.52: an isomorphism on positions. So (8.1) on positions collapses down to its bottom row, viewed as a diagram in Set . Yet this is precisely the diagram from (8.15), whose limit is tree . So tree is the position-set of the limit of (8.1): we write ( 1 )  tree .

So, as we said above, ( 1 ) is the set carrying the terminal coalgebra of , but we prefer to think of it as the set of -trees, for it gives us a concrete way to realize the limit and its projections, as well as a natural interpretation of the directions of at each position.

## The directions of the cofree comonoid

Given that is the limit of (8.1), Example 5.36 also tells us how to compute its directions: the directions of a limit are the colimit of the directions. But every polynomial in the bottom row of (8.1) has an empty direction-set, and there are no arrows between polynomials in the top row. So the directions of are given by a coproduct of the directions of each ⊳ .

More precisely, given a -tree ∈ tree whose stagepretree for ∈ N is ( ) , the direction-set [ ] is given by the following coproduct:

![[Polynomial Functors_ A Mathematical Theory of Interaction.assets/formula-0622.png]]

But by Definition 8.6, each ⊳ [ ( ) ] is the set of heightleaves of the ⊳ -pretree ( ) , which in turn is the stagepretree of . So its heightleaves coincide with the heightvertices of . Therefore, we can identify ⊳ [ ( ) ] with the set of heightvertices of ; we denote this set by vtx ( ) .

Since the coproduct above ranges over all ∈ N , it follows that [ ] is the set of all vertices in ; we denote this set by vtx ( ) . The on-directions function at of each projection → ⊳ from the limit must then be the canonical inclusion

![[Polynomial Functors_ A Mathematical Theory of Interaction.assets/formula-0623.png]]

sending heightleaves of ( ) to heightvertices of .

Here is an alternative way to think about the directions of and each ⊳ that will be helpful. A defining feature of a rooted tree is that its vertices are in bijection with its fi nite rooted paths: each vertex gives rise to a unique path to that vertex from the root, and every fi nite rooted path arises this way. So the directions of ⊳ at a given ⊳ -pretree correspond in turn to the rooted paths of that pretree leading to its heightleaves; indeed, each such direction is comprised of a sequence of directions of , which together specify a lengthrooted path up the pretree. Then for ∈ tree , the direction-set [ ] consists of every finite rooted path of .

Since only fi nite rooted paths correspond to vertices, all our paths will be assumed to be fi nite from here on out. This is our preferred way to think about directions in . When we wish to refer to what one might call an infinite (rooted) 'path,' we will instead call it a (rooted) ray .

## 8.1.2 Cofree comonoids as categories

We have now characterized the carrier of the comonoid T that will turn out to be cofree on ; but we have yet to describe the comonoid structure of T that carries. This structure will allow us to interpret T as a category, whose objects will be -trees and whose morphisms will be the vertices of each -tree. We could go ahead and describe this category right now, but as category theorists, let us show that the eraser and duplicator of T , as well as the comonoid laws that they satisfy, arise naturally from our construction of as the limit of (8.1).

## The eraser of the cofree comonoid

The eraser for a comonoid T carried by should be a lens of the form → y . Conveniently, since y appears in (8.1), its limit is already equipped with a canonical lens : → y , as seen in (8.19). This lens will turn out to be the eraser of the cofree comonoid on .

The eraser picks out a direction at each position of the carrier to be the identity morphism on that object. As each -position is a tree ∈ tree , and each [ ] -direction is a vertex of , the eraser should pick out a single vertex of every -tree. Even without looking at (8.19), you may already have your suspicions as to which vertex this will turn out to be, as there is, after all, only one sensible way to choose a canonical vertex that every -tree is guaranteed to have: choose its root. Indeed, Proposition 8.18 tells us that : → y sends each -tree ∈ tree to its stage-0 pretree on positions, stripping away everything except for the single unlabeled root of ; then on-directions, picks out the unique height-0 vertex of in vtx 0 ( ) ⊆ vtx ( ) , which is just its root.

2 As a reminder, due to the definition of a -tree , a -subtree of is still itself a -tree, whereas the ⊳ -pretree of is not a -tree unless the height of is strictly less than .

So in the category T , where morphisms out of objects are vertices of trees, the identity morphism on a tree is its root-in fact, we will henceforth denote the root of a -tree by id (so that vtx 0 ( ) = { id } ). Equivalently, we can identify id with the unique length-0 rooted path of : the empty rooted path , which starts and ends at the root. The rest of the categorical structure of T will be determined by its duplicator.

## The duplicator of the cofree comonoid

The duplicator for a comonoid T carried by should be a lens : → ⊳ . But before we can specify such a lens, we need to fi gure out what kind of polynomial ⊳ is.

Here is where our work from Sections 6.3.2 and 6.3.3 comes in handy: since the diagram in (8.1) is connected, its limit is a connected limit, and we showed in Theorem 6.79 that ⊳ preserves connected limits. Therefore ⊳ is itself the limit of some diagram, and a morphism to ⊳ is just a cone over that diagram.

Which diagram is it? First, we can use the fact that ⊳ preserves connected limits on the left to expand ⊳ as the limit of the following diagram, where we have applied the functor -⊳ to the diagram from (8.1):

![[Polynomial Functors_ A Mathematical Theory of Interaction.assets/figure-0306.png]]

But we have ⊳ ⊳ 1 ⊳  ⊳ ⊳ 1 , so we can simplify this diagram like so:

![[Polynomial Functors_ A Mathematical Theory of Interaction.assets/figure-0307.png]]

(This works because according to Proposition 6.67, ⊳ actually preserves all limits on the left-including the terminal object 1 .) So ⊳ is the limit of (8.26).

Then we use the fact that ⊳ preserves connected limits on the right to expand each ⊳ ℓ ⊳ in (8.26) as the limit of the following:

![[Polynomial Functors_ A Mathematical Theory of Interaction.assets/figure-0308.png]]

So the limit of (8.26) is the limit of a larger diagram, where we have 'plugged in' a copy of (8.27) in place of each ⊳ ℓ ⊳ that appears.

It's worth being a little careful, though, when we draw this diagram: each ⊳ ℓ ⊳ in (8.26) appears with a lens to ⊳ ℓ ⊳ 1 , and we need to work out what arrow should go in its place. The lens in question is given by applying ⊳ ℓ ⊳ -to the unique lens → 1 . But 1 actually shows up in the lower left corner of (8.1), so the unique lens → 1 is just the projection from the limit of (8.1) to that 1 . Then once we apply the connected limit-preserving functor ⊳ ℓ ⊳ -to (8.1), yielding (8.27), we obtain the lens ⊳ ℓ ⊳ → ⊳ ℓ ⊳ 1 we desire as the projection from the limit of (8.27) to the ⊳ ℓ ⊳ 1 in the lower left corner. So if we want to replace each ⊳ ℓ ⊳ in (8.26) with a copy of (8.27), without changing the limit of the whole diagram, we should replace the arrow ⊳ ℓ ⊳ → ⊳ ℓ ⊳ 1 with an identity arrow from the ⊳ ℓ ⊳ 1 in the lower left corner of (8.27) to the ⊳ ℓ ⊳ 1 in (8.26). Of course, we can then collapse these identity arrows down without changing the limit, so the diagram we are left with should look like this:

![[Polynomial Functors_ A Mathematical Theory of Interaction.assets/figure-0309.png]]

.

Here the bottom row of (8.26) is still the bottom row of (8.28), but in the place of each ⊳ ℓ ⊳ in the top row, we have grafted in a copy of (8.27). Yet the limit of the diagram is preserved: the limit of (8.28) is still ⊳ . In summary, for purely formal reasons, Proposition 6.67 and Theorem 6.79 ensure that since the limit of (8.1) is , the limit of (8.28) is ⊳ .

## Verifying the comonoid laws

Putting together our constructions of the carrier, the eraser, and the duplicator of the T yields the following result.

�  Proposition 8.33. As defined above, , , is a polynomial comonoid corresponding to a category T characterized as follows.

- An object in T is a -tree in ∈ tree .
- A morphism emanating from is a rooted path in ; its codomain is the -subtree rooted at the end of the path.
- The identity morphism on is its empty rooted path.
- Composition is given by concatenating rooted paths: given a rooted path in and a rooted path in cod , the -subtree rooted at the end of , we identify with the corresponding path in that starts where ends, then concatenate the two paths in to obtain the composite morphism # , another rooted path in .

Proof. We have already shown how to construct the given carrier, eraser, and duplicator purely diagrammatically, and we have given them concrete interpretations in terms of -trees, their vertices, and their -subtrees. So it remains to verify that the category laws hold for T , or �  equivalently that the comonoid laws hold for , , . Again, our argument will be purely formal, although it is not too hard to see that our concrete characterization above in terms of -trees satisfies the laws for a category.

First, we verify the left erasure law: that

![[Polynomial Functors_ A Mathematical Theory of Interaction.assets/figure-0313.png]]

commutes. We know the lens ⊳ : ⊳ → y ⊳ is characterized by ( its components ) ⊳ 0 ⊳ → ⊳ : ⊳ ⊳ , a projection out of the limit ⊳ , for all ∈ N . Then by our construction of , each   composite ( ) # ⊳ 0 ⊳ → ⊳ : ⊳ is the component of equal to ( ) ( 0 + ) = → : ⊳ 0 ⊳ ⊳  ⊳ (this is just the commutativity of (8.32) in the case of ( ℓ , ) = ( 0 , ) ). Together, these characterize �  the composite lens # ⊳ : → y ⊳  as the lens whose components ( are the projections ) : → ⊳ from the limit. It follows �  from the universal property of that # ⊳ can only be the identity lens on . Hence the left erasure law holds.
